### Strategy

<!-- Report Page 108 -->
#### [DR E1-1] Transition plan for climate change mitigation

[E1-1-AR.1] As the largest integrated energy producer in the SEE region, we recognize climate change as one of the most important global challenges today and we support the goals set forth by the Paris Climate Change Agreement; we therefore developed a transition plan for climate change mitigation for 2030 ("Transition Plan") to align our strategy and business model with the transition to a sustainable economy, as follows:

For our strategic horizon 2030, all our three strategic pillars support the energy transition, while generating value for our business and respond to our customers' needs:

Transition to low and zero carbon, by developing new low-emission products

Grow Regional Gas, best represented by our deep-offshore gas flagship project, Neptun Deep;

Traditional business, for which we are improving the operational efficiency;

This Transition Plan is aligned with our Strategy 2030 which is enabled by a sound capital allocation policy. Our new low and zero carbon business activities are expected to account for around 35% (EUR ~3.7 bn) of the cumulated gross CAPEX earmarked for the period 2022-2030. OMV Petrom remains committed to reducing GHG emissions by 2030 (details in this chapter, DR E1-4 Targets). Based on the benchmarking of our 2030 GHG absolute emissions reduction targets against the IEA climate scenarios trajectories, our emission reductions are in line with limiting the global warming to 2 degrees (details in E1-1.16a). We are already progressing in implementing this Transition plan (details in this chapter, DR E1-1.16j and in the Strategy 2030 section under the Company chapter).

In the longer term, our company's ambition is to achieve Net Zero operations (Scopes 1 and 2) by 2050 through improvement of operational efficiency (including by constant monitoring of new technologies), increase in zero-carbon energy usage, as well as sustainable feedstock and carbon reduction measures.

In 2024, our updated Strategy 2030 reinforced our commitment to transform for a lower carbon future, with acceleration and increase of renewable energy and emobility targets as well as setting green hydrogen targets. For biofuels, we adjusted downwards our 2030 initial capacity target due to technology maturity level and market developments. For more details, see Strategy 2030 section under the Company chapter.

Our commitment to sustainability and our Strategy 2030 are supported in our business model as an integrated energy player (DR 42, General disclosures chapter). We operate across the entire energy value chain, from exploration and production of oil and gas, to refining and marketing of refined petroleum products, as well as to power generation and marketing of gas and power. Our 2030 strategic pillars also enhance vertical integration across the energy value chain, reducing both our own and our clients' carbon footprint. For instance, the renewable energy we generate will not only be sold to our clients, but also used in our own operations. Additionally, it will power our EV charging network and be converted into green hydrogen in our electrolyzers for use by our refinery.

<!-- Report Page 109 -->
Exposure to coal and oil and gas-related activities

Natural gas is expected to play a pivotal role as a transition fuel in the power mixxxv of Romania's, with more than 50% lower CO2 emissions than coal in power generation. A game-changer for OMV Petrom and Romania, Neptun Deep is foreseen to come on stream in 2027, increasing Romania's and the region's long-term security of energy supply, while contributing to a lower carbon economy through its low carbon intensity footprint. In addition, the flexibility of gas-fired power plants, such as Brazi, support the integration of additional renewable capacities into the power system.

In terms of our traditional oil and gas portfolio, by 2030, the production is expected to decline (<5% production decline / yearxxvi). However, looking at OMV Petrom's overall hydrocarbon production, after Neptun Deep comes on stream, the share of gas - the cleanest fossil fuel - is expected to increase to 70% (vs. 52% in 2024). With its low carbon intensity operations, below industry average, the Neptun Deep project will contribute to reducing Scope 1&2 carbon intensityxxvii in the E&P business division. Beyond 2030, we anticipate an overall reduction in oil and gas production. Throughout this period, we plan to meet our customers' evolving energy needs while advancing our sustainability goals.

[E1-1.14,16a, AR 2, AR 26, AR 27, E1-4.33] GHG emission reduction targets

OMV Petrom is committed to reducing its absolute GHG emissions by 2030: 30% reduction of Scope 1 and 2 emissions and 20% reduction of Scope 1-3 emissions (only category 11 from Scope 3), compared to the baseline year of
2019. (see details regarding our targets in DR E1-4 GHG
Emissions reduction targets).

In assessing our 2030 total GHG reduction targets' compatibility with the Paris Agreement objectivexxviii, we compared them to the approximate trajectories implied by International Energy Agency (IEA) scenarios.

XXV ANRE Annual Report 2022, page 92;

xxvi 2024-2030 average annual decline in traditional hydrocarbon production, before divestments (estimated at 10-15 kboed/d in 2030) and excluding Neptun Deep volumes (70 kboe/d in 2030); source: OMV Petrom website, Capital Markets Day 2024.

xxvii Carbon Intensity of operations (Scopes 1 and 2) is calculated as GHG emissions divided by production and expressed in t CO2e / toe

XXVill Paris Agreement goal: limiting global temperature rise this century well below 2 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels and to pursue efforts to limit the temperature increase even further to 1.5 degrees Celsius.

In the sectoral report on The Oil and Gas Industry in Net Zero Transitions (2023), IEA states that globally, Scopes 1 and 2 emissions from oil and gas operations should fall by more than 60% in the NZE scenario by 2030 and by 35% in the APS (Announced Pledges Scenario) scenario versus
<!-- Report Page 110 -->
2022. IEA acknowledges however that achieving these
targets could be challenging for companies which have already undertaken major emissions reduction efforts in the past. OMV Petrom is committed to a reduction of Scope 1&2 emissions of 30% by 2030 (vs. 2019) which places our target closer to the APS reduction trajectory (and above NZE reduction trajectory).

According to the benchmarking analysis performed with the two IEA reports mentioned above, our 2030 GHG reduction targets are compatible with the Paris Agreement's goal of limiting global warming to below 2℃, however our targets are situated above a trajectory compatible with limiting the global temperature increase to 1.5℃. In the long term, OMV Petrom is committed to achieving Net Zero operations (Scopes 1 and 2) in 2050, in line with the IEA NZE by 2050 sectoral scenario.

Currently, there are no sectoral oil and gas pathways defined through public policies and no sectoral sciencebased target-setting methodologies which would facilitate a more precise evaluation of the alignment of GHG reduction targets with the Paris Agreement goal. Provided new guidance and methodologies become available, OMV

IEA World Energy Outlook (2024) describes three scenarios regarding global emissions and the associated temperature increase in each scenario until 2050: STEPS, APS and NZE.xxix OMV Petrom's GHG Scope 1-3 target reduction of - 20% in 2030 vs. 2019 is positioned between the NZE reduction trajectory (associated with 1.5℃ temperature increase) and APS (1.7℃ temperature increase). In our analysis, the percentage decrease of the global GHG emissions between 2019 and 2030 in each of the IEA scenariosxxx was used as proxy for the percentage decrease needed in any company's Scope 1-3 GHG emissions (so that the company's emissions trajectory can be compared to the corresponding temperature trajectory).

Petrom will revisit the 1.5℃ compatibility assessment and adjust related conclusions as needed.

[E1-1.16b, cross-reference to E1-4.34f, AR 30a-c] Decarbonization levers

To achieve our 2030 strategic GHG total emission reduction targets, OMV Petrom has identified climate change mitigation actions across its entire value chain (details in DR E1-3 Actions), aggregated in the decarbonization levers described below. These decarbonization levers group the investments identified as part of OMV Petrom's mid-term planning toward the OMV Petrom Strategy 2030 and its climate targets.

Lower fossil fuels sales: main decarbonization lever for the reduction of absolute Scope 3 emissions, by lowering

xxix Stated Policies Scenario (STEPS), corresponding to a temperature increase of more than 1.9℃ by 2050; Announced Pledges Scenario (APS) corresponding to a temperature increase of around 1.7℃ in 2050; Net Zero Emissions by 2050 Scenario (NZE) corresponding to a temperature increase of around 1.5℃, with peak temperature rise being less than 1.6 °C around 2040 before falling back to below 1.5 ℃ by 2100.

XXX We used 2019 as baseline year for computing 2030 reduction percentage: - 30% in the NZE scenario, -11% in APS and +1% in STEPS.

fossil fuels sold to customers in Romania and in the region; it also contributes to the improvement of carbon intensity of energy supply Scope 1, 2, 3.

Improve operational efficiency: this decarbonization lever includes initiatives aiming at optimizing and decarbonizing our operational processes by increasing energy efficiency, electrifying operations (green electricity generation from small on-site photovoltaic systems, generation of heat/cool from waste), reducing flaring and venting, and reducing methane emissions through leak detection and improvements to asset integrity. All these key actions contribute to reducing the absolute Scope 1&2 emissions (including to reduce methane emissions) and to improve the carbon intensity of energy supply Scope 1, 2, 3 indicator.

Increase in zero carbon energy sales: with impact on the achievement of our carbon intensity of energy supply

<!-- Report Page 111 -->
(Scope 1, 2, 3) target. This will be achieved by increasing sales of sustainable fuels and renewable power. Additionally, we are assessing the potential for other new low and zero carbon energy technologies (including hydrogen, geothermal energy, and biogas).

Portfolio changes: implementing strategic portfolio changes through acquisitions and investments, decommissioning, divesting assets, and optimizing our oil and gas portfolio focusing on gas as a transition fuel will help us reduce emissions and achieve our climate targets.

Neutralization measures e.g. CCS: GHG emission reduction measures such as using Carbon Capture and Storage (CCS) technologies will play a key role in decarbonization. We target to store around 2 million tons of CO2 in 2030.


[E1-1.16c, E1-3.29c (reference to EU Taxonomy KPI), E1- 3.AR 21-AR 22] Investments and funding supporting the implementation of the Transition Plan. To support OMV Petrom's climate targets, the company allocated approximately RON 18 bn gross for the period 2024-2030 (more details in this chapter, DR E1-3 Actions).

[E1-1.16d] Locked-in emissions refer to future GHG emissions that are likely to be caused by OMV Petrom's active and firmly planned key assets as well as the GHG emissions coming from the products sold. OMV Petrom's key assets regarding their locked-in emissions include the following assets:

Petrobrazi refinery

Brazi power plant

Exploration & Production assets

Petrobrazi refinery has an annual refining capacity of 4.5 mn tons/year of crude oil. The GHG emissions generated by Petrobrazi consist mainly of emissions from processing of crude oil into a wide range of petroleum products (gasoline, diesel, LPG, aviation fuel and other petroleum products).

Brazi power plant is a combined cycle power plant with a total capacity of 860 MW, allowing to cover approximately 10% of Romania's electricity generation mix. Its locked-in emissions are directly related to the gas consumption for electricity generation over its lifetime.

In E&P, we operate 149 commercial oil and natural gas fields in Romania, among which the Domino and Pelican South offshore gas fields from the Neptun Deep block. The emissions associated with E&P assets are from thermal and electrical production, flaring, venting, fugitive emissions and other specific processes.

The locked-in emissions associated with the products OMV Petrom sells are represented by Scope 3, Category 11, in line with the targets set for 2030. These emissions are

directly related to the combustion of the sold oil and gas products for energy use.

<!-- Report Page 112 -->
The locked-in emissions stemming from our key assets and products do not jeopardize the achievement of our emission targets, as their emissions profile over the coming years is accounted for in our Strategy 2030 (see details on decarbonization levers and key actions that support the achievement of our GHG targets 2030 in section E1-1.16b)

[E1-1.16e, E1-3.29c.iii] More than half of planned Sustainability CAPEX will go to zero carbon products. Planned projects considered within the Increase in zerocarbon energy sales lever mostly comprise of activities that have been aligned in 2023 and 2024.

[E1-1.16f] Significant CAPEX invested in 2024 related to oiland gas related economic activities amounted to RON 4.9 bn, of which RON 0.1 bn represent Sustainability CAPEX.

[E1-1.16g] As an oil and gas company, OMV Petrom is excluded from EU Paris-aligned benchmarks.

[E1-1.16h] Our Transition Plan for climate change mitigation is aligned with our Strategy 2030. It sets our ambition to be at the forefront of the energy transition ("Transition Plan") in Romania and the SEE region: lowering our carbon footprint while serving the society's increasing energy demand and need for energy security.

Our decarbonization plan is reflected in total GHG reduction targets for 2030 (-30% Scope 1 and 2 emissions, -20% Scope 1-3 emissions, -20% Carbon Intensity of Energy

Supply, compared to baseline year 2019) (for details regarding the methodologies used to define our targets, refer to DR E1-4 under this chapter). Additionally, we aim to achieve Net Zero operations (Scopes 1 and 2) by 2050.

Our ambition to decrease our carbon footprint is supported by our financial frame. By 2030, the company's portfolio is being expanded to include new low and zero carbon business activities, which together will account for around 35% of cumulated gross CAPEX (EUR 3.7 bn) for the 2022 \- 2030 period. Most of this CAPEX will have been spent in the period 2025 - 2030.

[E1-1.16i] Set forth in 2021, our Strategy 2030, which included our Transition Plan, was updated by the Executive Board and reapproved by our Executive Board and Supervisory Board in 2024.

In addition, all Transition Plan related disclosures (included in this report) are approved by the OMV Petrom Executive Board and Supervisory Board within the overall approval process for the 2024 Annual Report.

[E1-1.16j] The progress made in implementing our Transition Plan can be best recognized by looking at the implementation progress of our Strategy 2030 (details in chapter Strategy 2030), especially for the Transition to low and zero carbon business. Our ambitions in the low and zero carbon business are backed by investments (35%) over the course of the decade (EUR 3.7 bn gross between 2022 2030), out of which EUR 0.4 bn were already spent in the period 2022 - 2024.

Our 2030 GHG reduction targets and Net Zero operations in 2050 ambition remain unchanged, reinforcing our commitment to mitigate climate change (more details in Disclosure Requirement E1-4). In 2024, OMV Petrom continued to improve its energy and operational efficiency, reducing absolute Scope 1 and 2 emissions by 17% compared to 2019. During the same period, the E&P methane intensity decreased by approximately 80% along with significant reductions in flaring and venting. Scope 1-3 emissions were reduced by 11% compared to 2019, driven by lower gas sales and increased operational efficiency.

#### [ESRS 2 SBM-3] Interaction with strategy and business model

[E1-SBM-3.18] OMV Petrom's overall risk management process also covers climate change-related risks. These are analysed and addressed in a systematic way, according to adopted sustainability-related policies and targets. Climate change risk identification and assessment at OMV Petrom
<!-- Report Page 113 -->

consider various sources of uncertainty associated with physical and transition risks for our own operations and along the value chain.

As an integrated oil and gas company, OMV Petrom's business and its key assets are more exposed to transition risks in 2030 and beyond, rather than physical risks. Following the double materiality assessment, OMV Petrom has identified the following material climate-related transition risks:

Not achieving emissions reduction targets for own operations and products portfolio could result in penalties / fines / carbon taxes and reputational damage;

Decoupled energy transition pace (market trends vs. EU targets);

Disruptions in the downstream value chains.

[E1-SBM-3.19a-b, E1-SBM-3.19 AR 6; E1-SBM-3.19.AR7a] OMV Petrom's resilience framework for managing potential climate change crises or unpredictable threats adheres to the principles of assessment, testing, monitoring, and continuous improvement. This framework includes a stress test based on a "net zero emissions by 2050" scenario to identify the challenges OMV Petrom may face before 2030 related to the energy transition, including the risk of stranded assets, and to develop the necessary capabilities to address these challenges. The assumptions for this scenario are consistent with the IEA Net Zero Emissions (NZE) scenario, where available. Details about the macroeconomic trends and energy consumption and mix are disclosed in the Business Environment chapter of the Annual Report 2024.

[E1-SBM-3.19b-c, E1-SBM-3.19.AR7b-c, E1-SBM3.19.AR8a] The resilience analysis was performed in line with the consolidated financial statements prepared in accordance with IFRS as of December 31st, 2024. Further details can be found in the Note 2 to the consolidated financial statements for the year end December 31, 2024, in the Annual Report 2024.

The critical assumptions for a low carbon and resilient economy were set for OMV Petrom using sensitivities calculated based on the IEA Net Zero Emissions (NZE) price assumptions. Further details about time horizons and financial effects, as well as the mitigation measures and resources needed, can be found in the Note 2 to the consolidated financial statements for the year end December 31, 2024, in the Annual Report 2024.

Details about the uncertainties of the resilience analysis and to what extent the assets and business activities at risk are

considered within the definition of the OMV Petrom's 2030 strategy, investment decisions, and current and planned mitigation actions can be found in the Note 2 to the consolidated financial statements for the year end December 31, 2024, in the Annual Report 2024.

[E1-SBM-3.19.AR8b] OMV Petrom has committed to addressing climate change and reducing the carbon footprint of its operations and portfolio by 2030, with details provided in E1-4 section of SS 2024, supporting its ambition to achieve net-zero operations by 2050 (Scopes 1 and 2). While energy transition poses risks to oil and gas companies, it also triggers increases in demand for green energy which lead to investment opportunities across the value chain. OMV Petrom is well positioned to take advantage of these opportunities, while managing transition risks.

OMV Petrom continues to be in close dialogue with financial institutions and the investor community regarding its transformation toward net zero operations by 2050 and the necessary steps to follow the decarbonization pathway. OMV Petrom reports on its ESG performance and provides insights on its active approach to shaping its business strategy to ensure alignment with sustainability climate goals. This also supports access to attractive debt and equity financing.

While aligning with ESRS requirements and enhancing sustainability practices, OMV Petrom is targeting a broad diversification of its investor base. OMV Petrom fosters investor confidence and assesses ESG-aligned funding options to ensure access to funding and liquidity at attractive rates, if needed, in line with market developments.

##### Impact, Risk and opportunity management [DR E1-2] Policies
<!-- Report Page 114 -->

[E1-2.24,.25. E1-MDR-P] Key objectives addressing climate change mitigation and energy IROs include continuously improving carbon efficiency, supporting and accelerating the energy transition and achieving net-zero operations by 2050. We actively engage with stakeholders to develop and comply with the highest operating standards. Our monitoring process ensures transparency and continuous improvement in our sustainability efforts, aligning with our broader commitment to building a sustainable world for everyone.

Furthermore, climate change mitigation and energy efficiency are addressed within our company through policies that support the development and implementation of activities which are aligned with an inside-out perspective of reducing impacts on climate and enhancing the efficiency of

our operations. In this sense, our material impacts, risks and opportunities are primarily addressed by the following policies:

#### OMV Petrom Group Code of Conduct

[MDR-P-65a] The OMV Petrom Group Code of Conduct serves as a central guideline applicable to all our employees, business partners, and representatives of OMV Petrom Group. It lays out OMV Petrom's specific commitments on transition to low carbon business, run responsible operations, foster people and communities, ethical business practices, corporate values and existing reporting channels. The policy promotes sustainable and responsible behavior toward employees, the environment, and society, ensuring comprehensive adherence across the organization. [MDR-P-65b]. The Code of Conduct is applicable across the OMV Petrom Group, its consolidated subsidiaries and its supply chain. Adherence to the Code of Conduct is mandatory for everyone working for OMV Petrom or acting on behalf of, with, and for OMV Petrom. All business partners, and other individuals acting on behalf of OMV Petrom must comply with all relevant laws and regulations and follow our Code of Conduct or equivalent standards if they have their own adequate regulations in place. [MDR-P-65c]. The Executive Board of OMV Petrom assumes accountability for implementing the Code of Conduct. The fundamental values established in the Code of Conduct are guided by recognized best practices and international standards such as the goals set forth by the Paris Agreement, the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), the International Labour Organization (ILO), the UN Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights, and by adherence to the United Nations (UN) Global Compact [MDR-P-65d]. Key internal stakeholders are directly engaged in setting the policy [MDR-P-65e]. The Code of Conduct is publicly accessible on the corporate website. The policy is communicated through internal channels to ensure all employees are aware of and understand its contents [MDR-P-65f]. An updated version of the Code of Conduct was approved by the Executive Board in December 2024 and will be made publicly available beginning 2025.

#### Human rights Policy Statement

[MDR-P 65a] The policy outlines the company's commitment to upholding human rights across its business operations and supply chain. The Policy is aimed at advancing the company's positive impact related to creating opportunities for communities around suppliers and own operations, and at preventing the negative impacts of community dependence on OMV Petrom. This commitment is a guiding principle for handling human rights issues in

daily business activities, encompassing specific obligations like workers' rights, protective measures, vulnerable groups, environmental and climate change effects. The policy also details the company's due diligence process, grievance management, awareness, and communication strategies. [MDR-P-65b, f] The Policy Statement is applicable across the OMV Petrom Group, its consolidated subsidiaries and its supply chain and it is accessible through the company's internal regulatory platform and our corporate website.

[MDR-P-65c] The most senior level in OMV Petrom that is accountable for the implementation of the policy are the EB members. Business Heads (General Managers, VPs or Asset Managers)] hold responsibility for its implementation. [MDR-P-65e] The process for defining policies is informed by multiple internal stakeholders and their expectations in relation to the company.

### HSSE Directive and HSSE Policy

MDR-P-65a]. The HSSE Directive establishes the principles and rules for managing Health, Safety, Security & Resilience, and Environment (HSSE) across all stages of the company's business and activities, including capital projects and mergers and acquisitions. It serves as a framework for managing HSSE aspects with the aim of safeguarding people, the environment, assets, and the company's reputation, in accordance with OMV Petrom's HSSE Policy and its vision and mission of "Committed to Zero Harm - Protect People, Environment and Assets" The HSSE Policy states high level objective for OMV Petrom's approach in the areas of health, safety and environment. [MDR-P-65b]. The directive covers OMV Petrom Group [MDR-P-65c]. The directive lays down minimum expectations and requirements for different levels of functions, such as all staff, line management, senior management, corporate and divisional HSSE functions [MDR-P-65f].The Directive's requirements are communicated through internal channels, including training when major revision appears.

### GHG Management Framework

[MDR-P-65a]. The Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Management Framework outlines the commitment to achieving net-zero operational emissions by 2050, covering all GHG scopes, with specific targets set for 2025 and 2030. It includes systematic monitoring, reporting, and auditing of emissions, ensuring that operations are screened for GHG reduction and energy efficiency opportunities, implementing all financially viable measures. The framework mandates the quantification and documentation of impacts on GHG emissions (Scope 1-3) and energy efficiency potential, with a

<!-- Report Page 115 -->
commitment to achieving net-zero for operations (i.e. Scope 1-2) by 2050 and developing a pathway for Scope 3. It also addresses the identification, analysis, and evaluation of climate change risks and opportunities, following the HSSE Risk Management Standard, and ensures that all climaterelated risks are assessed in investments and M&A activities. The overarching objective is to systematically reduce emissions, enhance energy efficiency, and manage climate-related risks and opportunities through rigorous monitoring and adherence to best practices. [MDR-P 65b] The framework is applicable at OMV Petrom Group. [MDR-P 65c] Specific responsibilities are laid down for line management and senior management. [MDR-P e] Input and interests of key internal stakeholders were considered during the setting of the policy. [MDR-P 65e] The document is made available to the employees through the internal regulations' platform on company's intranet. For other interested parties such as authorities and auditors, the document is made available on request.

### Environmental Management Standard

[MDR-P 65a] The OMV Petrom Environmental Management Standard mandates that all its businesses and activities use energy responsibly, conserve primary energy resources, and implement energy management plans that are consistent with ISO 50001. It stipulates the assessment of environmental impacts and risks, ensuring adherence to performance requirements related to energy use, emissions, water use and discharge, raw materials, waste management, hazardous substances, and biodiversity protection. Key objectives include identifying and mitigating environmental impacts and risks, systematically monitoring and documenting GHG emissions, setting targets and KPIs for effective GHG management, and developing GHG forecasts aligned with business plans. The policy addresses material impacts, risks, and opportunities related to climate change and energy efficiency, requiring the implementation of financially viable carbon reduction measures. The process for monitoring includes systematic assessment and screening of GHG-related reduction risks and opportunities, ensuring compliance with the highest environmental standards. [MDR-P 65b] The Standard applies to all OMV Petrom group businesses and activities.[MDR-P 65c] Line management is responsible for the effective implementation of this standard in their operations/businesses. Asset Development Managers and Project Managers are responsible for the implementation of this standard in all relevant projects. [MDR-P-65d] For sites/operations holding a certificate according to ISO 14001 the requirement of the standard is covered by the certification. [MDR-P-65e] Within the setting of the Environmental Management Standard, all key internal stakeholders were consulted and their feedback

was taken into consideration.[MDR-P 65e] The document is made available to the employees through the company's internal regulations platform. For other interested parties such as authorities and auditors, the document is made available on request

### HSSE Risk Management Standard

[MDR-P 65a] The HSSE Risk Management Standard sets the framework and minimum requirements for managing health, safety, security, and environmental risks. It allows OMV Petrom to seize opportunities while prioritizing the protection of people, the environment, and assets, thereby maintaining business integrity. The standard outlines a process for identifying, assessing, controlling, documenting, and communicating HSSE risks.

### Enterprise-Wide Risk Management Standard (EWRM)

[MDR-P 65a] The aim of this standard is to define and support OMV Petrom's EWRM activities through: (1) clear, continuous and consistent risk management process across OMV Petrom Group, from the operational level to the OMV Petrom Executive Board), (2) documentation of clear accountability and ownership of risk and EWRM activities and (3) explanation of the relationship between EWRM and related directive, procedures and committees. This standard addresses all major risks within our organization.

[MDR-P b-f] For further information regarding the Enterprise Wide Risk Management Standard, please refer to Economic value chapter.

### [DR E1-3] Actions and resources in relation to climate change policies

[E1-3.29a, b, ESRS 2 MDR-A68 a-c] To prevent and, wherever applicable, mitigate the impacts and risks identified in relation to Climate change, OMV Petrom has defined the following key actions, as described in the table below. By increasing zero carbon sales, implementing neutralization measures such as Carbon Capture and Storage (CCS), and improving operational efficiency, we address the negative

impacts related to GHG emissions from our operations and products sold. These efforts help mitigate the emissions and energy consumption that result inherent to the oil and gas industry. This has a positive impact on reducing GHG emissions through the energy transition, supporting society's shift from a linear to a sustainable economy by offering diversified products with a lower carbon footprint.

[ESRS 2 MDR-A.69a; AR21] Projects selection and prioritization could be influenced also by regulatory and market developments and might end up in different allocation between technologies. The overall planning of relevant investments for the implementation of the action plan includes non reimbursable financial support.

[E1-3.29 c; ESRS 2 MDR-A69b] The CAPEX required to implement the actions taken in 2024 as shown in the table below is included in the Group total CAPEX; for the Group's total CAPEX and its reconciliation to the investments shown in the cash flow statement, refer to Director's Report chapter, section "Capital Expenditure (CAPEX)" and the Consolidated Financial Statements, Consolidated statement of cash flows".
<!-- Report Page 116 -->

Key actions are defined as those requiring CAPEX of EUR > 5 mn for their implementation. CAPEX includes additions to property, plant and equipment and to intangible assets (including IFRS 16 right-of-use assets) and expenditures for acquisitions as well as equity-accounted investments and other interest for pre-defined Sustainability CAPEX categories. Decommissioning assets, government grants and other additions that by definition are not considered capital expenditure are not included in gross CAPEX figures. Within the boundaries of applicable accounting standards, expenditure incurred during project implementation is generally capitalized, thus included in the CAPEX figures. Figures are not validated by external bodies. For the material topic Climate change, the key actions mainly refer to activities in Europe, the majority of them in Romania.

Due to the threshold of > EUR 5 mn per project, the CAPEX figures in the table below do not represent the total CAPEX OMV Petrom allocated to addressing climate mitigation.

<!-- Report Page 120 -->

[MDR-T-80a, e ] Mitigating climate change is a strategic priority for our business. To this end, we have set decarbonization targets to support our strategic objective to decarbonize our operations and reduce our environmental impact on climate. The decarbonization targets are part of OMV Petrom's Strategy 2030, reflecting our commitment to driving the energy transition in Southeastern Europe (SEE) and achieving Net Zero operations by 2050. This commitment and subsequent targets are in line with the objectives of our GHG Management Framework and Code of Conduct.

[MDR-T 80 f, g] While the methodology for targets setting did not rely on a science-based approach, sectoral decarbonization pathways or climate scenarios, we have considered future market and regulatory developments and taken into consideration business-related parameters that are relevant for our strategic development, such as:

Regulatory and legal context: the main regulatory requirements identified at EU level, which have a direct impact on our business, are the EU's decarbonization targets, such as the ones set for the transport sector. These are part of the broader European Green Deal and Fit for 55 package which aim to reduce the net greenhouse gas emissions by at least 55% by 2030, compared to 1990 levels.

Shareholder needs for setting GHG targets that balance sustainable long-term value by transitioning to cleaner energy sources while ensuring effective capital allocation and robust financial performance; specific considerations for setting GHG emissions reduction targets for:

Scope 1&2: business target to decarbonize own operations by 2030 (relying on several emissions reduction and energy efficiency initiatives), supporting the company's long-term ambition of Net Zero operations by 2050

Scopes 1-3 target: CCS investments play a key role in achieving our overall carbon footprint reduction.

Market research for prognosis of products demands, with specific considerations for setting GHG emissions reduction targets for:

Scope 3: portfolio development to meet our clients' demand (ensure continuity of supply for the strong demand forecasted until 2030) while helping our customers to decarbonize, thereby reducing Scope 3 emissions over time;

High level review of main GHG targets in comparison with industry peers (including by reviewing investments in key initiatives, such as CCS)

Regulatory and legal context, market research for prognosis of products demands and shareholders needs formed the significant assumptions for our operational scenarios and related decarbonization targets setting. The assumptions for target setting were based on market development, internal estimates and a projected slower energy transition in Southeastern Europe compared to Western Europe, while still ensuring security of supply for our market.

The final targets were developed bottom-up (by calculating potential emissions reduction from key decarbonization projects) and adjusted with a top-down ambition established by OMV Petrom leadership.

[E1-4 34f, AR30a] The contribution of the decarbonization levers to achieve the GHG emissions reduction target is covered in sections E1-1 Transition Plan and E1-3 Key actions.

[AR 30b] OMV Petrom tested an innovative technology for carbon capture and utilization at the Petrobrazi refinery. OMV Petrom is a partner in an ambitious European project to address CO2 emissions through carbon capture and conversion technologies. Over a five-month period in 2024, we captured the CO2 from flue gasses released by the cogeneration unit, further converting it electrochemically into a marketable product.

[AR 30c] OMV Petrom's emission reduction activities are organized into decarbonization levers, as detailed above. These levers encompass investments identified during OMV Petrom's mid-term planning aligned with the Strategy 2030 and its climate objectives. Scenario analysis forms the foundation for developing this strategy, setting targets, and planning corresponding investments. The business cases for these investments are evaluated based on a base case scenario, informed by the IEA's Announced Pledges Scenario (APS) and supplemented by other external and internal market analyses. Furthermore, these investments are subjected to a stress test aligned with a "net zero emissions by 2050" scenario, primarily based on the IEA's Net Zero Emissions by 2050 (NZE) scenario.

[E1-4.34c, E1-4.AR 25a-b] Following an internal analysis on operational development in the last years, we identified an operational year that presents a business-as-usual operational scenario that was not influenced by deviations in the economic and market patterns generated by international crises such as the COVID-19 pandemic. Taking into consideration an internal stakeholder review and following benchmarking examples, we have elected to use 2019 as the baseline year for our decarbonization targets, as it best represents the business-as-usual scenario.To reflect the most up-to-date scientific understanding of our emissions, we have updated our calculations by aligning with the latest Global Warming Potentials (GWP) from IPCC's Sixth Assessment Report (AR6). When targets were approved, Scope 1 GHG emissions were calculated using the GWP from AR4, in line with the internal rules. Consequently, baseline year value and monitoring performance against targets were updated to AR6. However, for comparability of our reported emissions, we disclose the baseline value in both scenarios, AR4 and AR6. The baseline value for Scopes 1, 2 and 3 GHG emissions using AR6 is 29.1 million tCO2e (2019 AR4: 28.9 million tCO2e).

In 2024, no base year value recalculation was triggered. The base year will remain unchanged unless significant changes in target or reporting boundaries occur. The updating of the GWPs is not considered a recalculation of the base year value as this update is a recurrent activity, in line with the latest scientific data.

Base year re-calculation: Based on applicable internal rules, should a significant change in GHG emissions occur due to mergers/acquisitions/divestments, a re-calculation of the base year will be done for the GHG reduction targets achievement assessment (including LTIP, senior management remuneration, and Group incentive plans annual bonus target achievement). A significant change means that the cumulative effect of mergers/acquisitions/divestments in the reporting year represents a higher effect than 5% on baseline year absolute Scope 1-3 emissions which are in scope of the GHG targets.

[MDR-T-i ] Any changes in targets and corresponding metrics or underlying measurement methodologies will be transparently disclosed on an annual basis. An explanation of the rationale for these changes and their effect on comparability will also be provided, ensuring clarity and consistency in reporting.

Additional notes regarding our methodology for specific targets

Scope 1-2 GHG emissions target - which refers to OMV Petrom's Scope 1 direct & Scope 2 (market-based) indirect GHG emissions.

[MDR-T-80b] The defined target level to achieve a 30% absolute GHG emissions reduction by 2030, measured in tCO2e (carbon dioxide equivalent), providing a clear and quantifiable benchmark for progress.

[MDR-T-80c] The scope includes OMV Petrom's own operations (Scope 1) and upstream value chain energy generation (Scope 2). We count the Scope 1 direct GHG emissions and Scope 2 indirect GHG emissions for fully owned assets and assets where OMV Petrom's interest is less than 100% but more than 50%, and where OMV Petrom operates a joint venture. The target excludes assets which are not majority owned by OMV Petrom and assets over which the company has no operational control. Geographically, the targets cover all assets and activities of OMV Petrom, ensuring comprehensive coverage of the company's environmental impact.

[MDR-T-80d] The baseline year is 2019, with a recorded baseline value of 4.7 million tCO2e, encompassing Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions as per IPCC AR6 guidelines (2019 AR4: 4.5 million tCO2e). This baseline provides a reference point from which progress is measured and evaluated over time.

<!-- Report Page 121 -->
[MDR-T-80f] Achieving Net-zero operations (Scopes 1 and
removals (acc. to ESRS definitions), avoided emissions, carbon credits or any other feasible neutralization measure. GHG removals (acc. to ESRS definitions), avoided emissions and carbon credits are excluded as a means of achieving 2030 GHG emission reduction targets.

[MDR-T-80h] The GHG target was proposed by HSSE and Strategy, discussed with the Investor Relations (IR) team, Supervisory Board (SB), Executive Board (EB) and other relevant business functions. The target was approved by OMV Petrom EB and SB.

[MDR-T-80j] The responsibility for target definition and performance monitoring against disclosed targets lies with OMV Petrom corporate HSSE-Environmental Management and CFO Controlling, with an annual monitoring frequency.

## Scope 1-3 GHG emissions target

This target encompasses OMV Petrom's Scope 1 direct & Scope 2 (market-based) indirect & Scope 3 indirect GHG emissions.

[MDR-T-80b] By 2030, OMV Petrom aims to achieve a 20% absolute reduction in Scope 1-3 GHG emissions. The unit of measurement for this target is metric tons of CO2 equivalent (tCO2e).

[MDR-T-80c] The scope of the target includes OMV Petrom Group's own operations, upstream and downstream value chain. Specifically, it covers: Scope 1 direct GHG emissions and Scope 2 indirect GHG emissions for fully owned assets and assets where OMV Petrom's interest is less than 100% but more than 50%, and where OMV Petrom operates a joint venture and Scope 3 indirect GHG emissions from category 11 (use of sold products) to third parties on an equity production basis.

In 2024 the percentage of Scope 3 emissions, covered by the target, out of the total Scope 3 emissions is 89% (2019: 94%).

Scope 1&2 GHG emissions of assets not operated/majority owned by OMV Petrom, intracompany sales, and purely financial traded volumes are excluded.

[MDR-T-80d] The baseline year for these targets is 2019, with a baseline value of 29.1 million tCO2e, as per IPCC AR6 standards (2019 AR4: 28.9 million tCO2e).

[MDR-T-80f] Achieving Net-zero operations (Scope 1&2) by 2050 includes own measures as well as CCU/S, GHG removals, avoided emissions and carbon credits or any other feasible neutralization measure. Achieving Scope 3 GHG emission reduction includes own measures as well as CCU/S in the value chain and any additional established CCS capacity. GHG removals, avoided emissions and carbon credits are excluded as a means of achieving 2030 GHG emission reduction targets.

[MDR-T-80h] The target was proposed by HSSE and Strategy, discussed with Controlling, IR, EB, SB, and other relevant business functions. The target was approved by OMV Petrom EB and SB.

[MDR-T-80j] The responsibility for target definition and performance monitoring lies OMV Petrom corporate HSSEEnvironmental Management and CFO Controlling, with an annual monitoring frequency.

## E&P Methane Intensity

This indicator refers to the volume of methane emissions from our E&P oil and gas assets as a percentage of the volume of the total gas that goes to market.

[MDR-T-80b] OMV Petrom aims to achieve a methane intensity of less than 0.2%, measured in scm (standard cubic meters) CH4/ scm of gas sold by 2030.

[MDR-T-80c] The target encompasses E&P own activities, from exploration to production, including gas processing. As methane is a greenhouse gas, counted under our Scope 1, the methane estimation follows the same principle mentioned for Scope 1, also meaning that in scope for this target are methane emissions from fully owned assets and assets where OMV Petrom's interest is less than 100% but more than 50%, and where OMV Petrom operates a joint venture. Emissions from fugitives, venting, and incomplete combustion are included, while methane emissions from assets not operated or not majority owned by OMV Petrom, as well as exploration drilling activities, are excluded.

[MDR-T-f, g] The methane intensity reduction target set in 2021 stems from the need to reduce methane emissions in the oil and gas sector, now, also in line with EU requirements (EU methane regulation) and IEA expectations for the oil and gas sector. The approach for calculating the methane intensity is in line with the Oil and Gas Climate Initiative's (OGCI) methane intensity, calculated as the ratio of methane emissions to marketed gas sales. However, the actual methane emissions might be subject for an update, following the recent adoption of the EU Methane Regulation.

According to the legal requirements, the first methane measurement reports are to be available later, in 2025.

[MDR-T-h] This target was proposed by OMV Petrom corporate HSSE, discussed and agreed with SB, EB, Group Strategy and other relevant business function. The target was approved by OMV Petrom EB.

[MDR-T-j] The responsibility for target definition and performance monitoring against disclosed targets lies with OMV Petrom corporate HSSE-Environmental Management and E&P Emissions Management department, with an annual monitoring frequency.

### Carbon Intensity of Energy Supply

Carbon intensity of energy supply represents total GHG emissions (scope 1 & 2 & 3) divided by total energy content (of sold energy products: gas to energy, oil to energy, power and biofuels).

[MDR-T-80b, d] OMV Petrom has set a strategic target to reduce the carbon intensity of its energy supply by 20%. The target is measured in grams of CO2 equivalent per megajoule (gCO2e/MJ) and is relative to a baseline year of 2019, which had a carbon intensity of 70.7 gCO2e/MJ as per IPCC AR6 guidelines (AR4 2019: 70.2 gCO2e/MJ).

[MDR-T-80c] The scope of this target includes OMV Petrom's own operations, upstream value chain (energy generation), and downstream value chain (product use). Specifically, it covers Scope 1 direct GHG emissions, Scope 2 indirect GHG emissions, accounted as 100% for fully owned assets and for assets where the OMV Petrom's interest is less than 100% but more than 50%, and where the OMV Petrom's interest is 50% or less if OMV Petrom is the operator of a joint venture, and OMV Petrom (E&P,

R&M, G&P) Scope 3 indirect GHG emissions from category 11 (3.11) "use of sold products" to third parties on an equity production basis. Out of scope are Scope 1&2 GHG emissions of assets not operated/not majority owned by OMV Petrom, intracompany sales and purely financial traded volumes, or other Scope 3 indirect GHG emissions. The geographical coverage for this target is the entire OMV Petrom Group.

[MDR-T-80f, g] To achieve this target, OMV Petrom envisages to implement measures such as CCS in its operations and value chain, as well as to investigate other technologies. GHG removals, avoided emissions and carbon credits are excluded as a means of achieving 2030 GHG emission reduction targets.

[MDR-T-80h] The target was proposed by OMV Petrom corporate HSSE-Environmental Management and Strategy, discussed with IR, SB, EB and other relevant business functions. The target was approved by the OMV Petrom EB and SB.

[MDR-T-80j] The responsibility for target definition and performance against disclosed targets monitoring lies with OMV Petrom corporate HSSE-Environmental Management and CFO Controlling, with an annual monitoring frequency.

[E1-6.50, AR 40] All greenhouse gas data for Scope 1, Scope 2, and Scope 3 emissions is collected for activities which OMV Petrom operates or where OMV Petrom holds a stake of more than 50% and exerts a controlling influence. Operational control (fully consolidated companies) are companies over which OMV Petrom has full control according to IFRS requirements.

All third party sales are considered for Scope 3 category 10 and 11. When a company participates in joint operations, 100% of the respective company sales are accounted, however this value usually only represents its share in the joint operation. The exception to this is Scope 3 Category 15 (Investments), which follows the equity approach. The share of the investment's Scope 1, 2, and, where relevant, Scope 3 emissions are accounted for in this category. If an investment is made with a business partner in OMV Petrom's upstream or downstream value chain, the respective Scope 3 emissions are included in the appropriate category.

[E1-6.AR 39a, MDR-M.77a] OMV Petrom calculates its corporate carbon footprint (Scope 1, 2 and 3 emissions) following the principles, requirements and guidance provided by the GHG Protocol Corporate Standard (version 2004), the GHG Protocol Scope 2 Guidance (version 2015) and the GHG Protocol Corporate value chain (Scope 3) Accounting and Reporting Standard (Version 2011), expressed in tons CO2e.

We are following our GHG Management Framework standard (in line with GHG Protocol) and actively working to mitigate the GHG emissions along the value chain. To ensure that the approach for GHG accounting and reporting is properly implemented, we perform regular data quality checks.

<!-- Report Page 126 -->
[MDR-M.77b] The emissions value for the EU-ETS installations, covered by Scope 1, are verified by a thirdparty accredited company, according to legal requirements. Other metrics are not verified by an external body, except for our assurance provider.

[MDR-M.77c] Total GHG emissions: The total GHG emissions are calculated as the sum of 100% of gross Scope 1, 100% gross Scope 2 (market-based) emissions and 100% of gross Scope 3 GHG emissions (all categories relevant for oil and gas industry that cross the materiality threshold, as defined in our internal regulation). The relevant categories are listed as metrics. Some data included in this metric undergoes verification by an external body when GHG emissions are regulated under an EU-ETS system.

[E1-6.AR 39c] Atmospheric emissions that contribute to global warming are specified in the Kyoto Protocol. There are six greenhouse gases covered by the Kyoto Protocol: carbon dioxide (CO2), methane (CH4), nitrous oxide (N2O), hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs), perfluorocarbons (PFCs), and sulfur hexafluoride (SF6). HFCs, PFCs and SF6 are of minor relevance for OMV Petrom. The global warming potential (GWP) is a relative scale which compares the gas in question to that of the same mass of carbon dioxide.

[E1-6.AR 39d] OMV Petrom applied the conversion factors defined by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), Fourth Assessment Report AR4, GWP values for 100-year time horizon. However, since 2024 has been applying the global warming potentials from the Sixth Assessment Report (AR6) to calculate CO2e emissions of non-CO2 gases) and API GHG Compendium.

[E1-6.47, AR 42c] The reporting periods of entities within OMV Petrom's value chain covered within this calculation do not differ from the OMV Petrom's general financial reporting dates. Therefore, no significant changes between the undertaking and its upstream and downstream value chain can be mentioned.

## Scope 1 GHG Emissions

[MDR-M.77c] Scope 1 emissions are defined as direct emissions from all sources under direct control of the company (owned or operated), such as stationary sources, mobile sources, process emissions, flaring/venting emissions, fugitive emissions. OMV Petrom's Scope 1 emissions (measured in tonnes of CO2eq) from the business divisions (R&M, G&P and E&P) come primarily from fuel combustion, process emissions flaring, venting and fugitive emissions.

[MDR-M.77a, E1-6.AR 39b] Scope 1 GHG emissions are calculated by multiplying activity data with emission factors. OMV Petrom uses emission factors from various sources, such as the IPPC (since 2024 has been applying the global warming potentials from the IPPC Sixth Assessment Report (AR6 - 100 years) to calculate CO2e emissions of non-CO2

gases) and API GHG Compendium. OMV Petrom includes the greenhouse gases CO, CH, and N O in its Scope 1 calculations. OMV Petrom is also subject to EU-ETS report in accordance with the EU-ETS methodology. Four of our operating installations were under the EU Emissions Trading Scheme (EU-ETS) at the end of 2024.

Activity data is derived from site-specific information, utilizing a combination of direct measurements, calculations, and estimations. When direct measurements or calculations are not feasible, estimations are used to determine the energy consumption. Potential limitations of the methodology include the accuracy and reliability of estimations when direct measurements and calculations are not feasible. Potential limitations include the accuracy of measurement devices, the reliability of estimation methods, variations in emission factors, and potential gaps in data collection from all relevant sources.

[MDR-M.77b] Some data included in this metric undergoes verification by an external body when GHG emissions are regulated under the EU-ETS system.

[MDR-M.77a, E1-6.AR 44] The percentage of Scope 1 GHG emissions from regulated emission trading schemes is calculated by dividing the sum of GHG emissions from EU ETS installations by the total Scope 1 GHG emissions. Limitations can arise when emissions are reported in the company's annual sustainability statement before the final verification report is issued by an external body due to differing reporting deadlines. Emission trading systems may also vary in terms of specific deadlines and processes, tailored to their respective regulatory environments. OMV Petrom doesn't own or control any non-EU ETS installation.

Biogenic CO2 emissions are calculated as CO2 released from the combustion or decomposition of organic materials, such as biomass and biofuels used or sold by OMV Petrom. This is reported separately referring to each scope of GHG emissions. Biogenic CO2 emissions not included in Scope 1 GHG emissions is derived by site-specific consumptions of renewable fuels, including biomass, and established emission factors published by IPCC.

## Scope 2 GHG Emissions

[MDR-M.77c] Scope 2 emissions are defined as indirect GHG emissions that occur at the point of energy generation (owned or operated by a third party) for electricity, heat, cooling or steam imported (i.e. purchased) for use on site. OMV Petrom's Scope 2 emissions (measured in tonnes of CO2eq) are those related to the production of the energy we

purchased for our operations. The Scope 2 emissions are primarily generated by R&M and E&P business segments.

[MDR-M.77a, E1-6.AR 39b] Scope 2 GHG emissions are calculated by multiplying activity data (MWh of energy purchased recorded and invoiced) with GHG emission factors from suppliers - energy mix or general (country) energy mix (International Energy Agency) of the locations if the supplier label is not available. Latest labels available are used.

## Scope 3 GHG Emissions

[MDR-M.77c] Scope 3 emissions are defined as other indirect emissions that are generated by our products or caused by supply chain activities. The metric is an aggregated emissions value of the relevant Scope 3 categories. OMV Petrom's most relevant Scope 3 emissions (measured in tonnes of CO2eq) result from the energy consumption by our customers and stakeholders, including the products use. Scope 3 emissions from the use and processing of sold products (excluding pure "trading margin" sales and intracompany sales) are included in the total Scope 3.

Biogenic CO2 emissions are calculated by measuring the CO2 released from the combustion or decomposition of organic materials, such as biomass and biofuels. This is reported separately referring to each scope of GHG emissions. The value of Biogenic CO2 emissions not included in Scope 3 GHG emissions is derived from energy sales from renewable sources such as biofuels, and established emission factors by IPCC.

[MDR-M.77a, E1-6.AR 39b, E1-6.AR 46g-i, ESRS 2-BP2.10b, ESRS 2-BP-2.10c] Scope 3 GHG emissions are measured using inputs from specific activities within OMV Petrom's upstream and downstream value chain. Emission factors associated with Category 1 - Purchased goods and services and Category 2 - Capital goods, are calculated using primary data obtained from suppliers or other value chain partners. In 2024, 1% of Scope 3.1 "Purchased goods and services" and 5% of Scope 3.2 "Capital goods" were calculated using data obtained from suppliers. This corresponds to less than 1% of total Scope 3 emissions. This metric is calculated as the share of Scope 3 emissions calculated using emission factors from the clients (category 1 & 2) divided by total Scope 3 emissions.

As for the emission factor used in calculating the remaining Scope 3 categories, OMV Petrom has made use of estimates. OMV Petrom calculates its carbon footprint (Scope 1, 2 and 3 emissions) following the principles,

requirements and guidance provided by the GHG Protocol Corporate Standard (version 2004), the GHG Protocol Scope 2 Guidance (version 2015) and the GHG Protocol Corporate value chain (Scope 3) Accounting and Reporting Standard (Version 2011). We use emission factors from different sources, such as the IPCC and Plastics Europe. Detailed information regarding the calculation methodology for each Scope 3 category is presented below.

Category 1 Purchased goods and services: The aggregated Scope 3.1 emissions related to emissions from purchased goods and services and thereof the share of emissions from feedstock is reported separately.

The calculation method for emissions combines an average data method for purchased refinery feedstock and a hybrid method for purchased goods and services, incorporating supplier-specific factors when available. Activity data examples include the net amount of refinery feedstock purchased, mass or volume of goods and services, and the amount spent on goods and services by product type. Emission factors used are based on the energy content of refinery feedstocks, the quantity of purchased goods and services, the economic value of these goods and services, and supplier-specific emission factors per unit of economic value. Using this method carries potential limitations such as inaccuracies in emission factors, especially for supplierspecific data, and variability in data quality across different suppliers and products. Additionally, relying on average data for feedstock might not capture specific emissions accurately. Differences in reporting standards and time lags in data availability can further affect the precision and reliability of the calculated emissions.

Category 2 Capital goods: Scope 3.2 covers upstream (i.e., cradle-to-gate) emissions from the production of capital goods purchased or acquired by OMV Petrom in the reporting year.

Emissions are calculated by multiplying activity data with emission factors. Activity data include amount spent on capital goods by product type. The emission factors used are those associated with the purchased goods & services per unit of economic value and supplier-specific emission factors for the purchased goods and services per unit of economic value.

Category 3 Fuel and energy-related activities: Scope 3.3 is defined as emissions related to the production of fuels and energy purchased and consumed by OMV Petrom in the reporting year that are not included in Scope 1 or Scope 2.

Emissions are calculated by multiplying activity data with emission factors. Activity data include amount of purchased and consumed fuels, electricity, heat, steam and cooling. The emission factors used are based on the average emission factors for upstream emissions per unit of consumption.

Category 5 Waste generated in operations: Scope 3.5 is defined as emissions from third-party disposal and treatment of waste generated within OMV Petrom's operations in the reporting year.

Emissions are calculated by multiplying activity data with emission factors. Activity data include total mass of waste generated in operations per disposal method. The emission factors used are based on the average emission factors based on waste disposal types.

Category 10 Processing of sold products: Scope 3.10 are defined as emissions from processing of sold intermediate products by third parties subsequent to sale by OMV Petrom. Pure trading margin sales as well as intracompany sales are excluded from category 10 and 11.

Emissions are calculated by multiplying activity data with emission factors. Activity data include mass or volume of sold intermediate products per product type and the process(es) involved in transforming or processing sold intermediate products into a usable state final product subsequent to sale by the reporting company. Emission factors used are based on the average emission factors for processes stages required to transform the sold intermediate product into a final product.

Category 11 Use of sold products: Scope 3 Category 11 are defined as emissions from the use of goods and services sold by OMV Petrom in the reporting year. Pure trading margin sales as well as intracompany sales are excluded from category 10 and 11.

Emissions are calculated by multiplying activity data with emission factors. Activity data include mass or volume of sold products per product type. Emission factors used are based on the average emission factors for use of sold products.

Category 15 Investments: Scope 3 Category 15 are defined as emissions associated with OMV Petrom's investments in the reporting year, not already included in scope 1 or scope 2 including emissions from subsidiaries and joint ventures where OMV Petrom does not have operational control. Emissions quantification follows the equity approach.

<!-- Report Page 127 -->
Justification for exclusion of certain Scope 3 categories

Category 4 "Upstream transportation and distribution" is excluded based on IPIECA guidelines, which suggest that upstream transportation and distribution emissions should not be counted separately, as the fuels used are already accounted for in Scope 3 Category 11, "Use of sold products". This prevents double counting.

Category 6 "Business travel" is excluded to avoid double counting because the fuels involved are included under Category 11 "Use of sold products" (excluded based on IPIECA guidelines).

Category 7 "Employee commuting" are excluded to avoid double counting because the fuels involved are included under Category 11 "Use of sold products" (excluded based on IPIECA guidelines).

Category 8 Upstream leased assets: OMV Petrom's leased assets such as offshore platforms and joint ventures are included in Scope 1 emissions. Thus, to prevent double counting these are not accounted for in OMV Petrom.

Category 9 "Downstream transportation and distribution" are excluded to avoid double counting because the fuels involved are included under Category 11 "Use of sold products" (excluded based on IPIECA guidelines).

Category 12 End-of-life treatment of sold products: OMV Petrom products, which are not used as fuels are accounted under Scope 3 category 10 "Processing of sold products". This category is not relevant for OMV Petrom.

Category 13 Downstream leased assets: Scope 3 Category 13 are defined as emissions from the operation of assets that are owned by OMV Petrom (acting as lessor) and leased to other entities in the reporting year that are not already included in scope 1 or scope 2. This category is applicable to lessors (i.e., companies that receive payments from lessees). Emissions of assets that are owned by OMV Petrom are included Scope 1 or 2. Therefore there are no emissions in this category.

Category 14 Franchises: Scope 3 Category 14 are defined as emissions from the operation of franchises not included in scope 1 or scope 2. A franchise is a business operating under a license to sell or distribute another company's goods or services within a certain location. OMV Petrom does not have any franchise activities.

[E1-7, 56b, E1-7-59 a,b] [E1-7, 56b, E1-7-59 a,b] The total amount of carbon credits outside of OMV Petrom's value chain that are due to be canceled in the future is 74,977 t of CO2e. All of these credits are based on existing contractual agreements.

MDR-M 77 a-c] The total amount of carbon credits canceled iduring the reporting year is determined by documenting the number of carbon credits officially retired or canceled within the year. This involves monitoring credit transactions and ensuring they meet regulatory and voluntary offset program requirements. The total value is reported separately for the share by type of project, the share of projects within the EU, and the share of carbon credits that qualify as corresponding adjustment. Potential limitations include the accuracy and completeness of the records, possible delays in the credit cancelation processing, and adherence to evolving regulatory standards.

The metric relating to the total carbon credits canceled in the reporting year according to recognized quality standard is determined by tracking and verifying the number of carbon credits that have been officially retired or canceled within the

reporting period, ensuring they align with established standards such as the Verified Carbon Standard (VCS) or the Gold Standard. This involves thorough documentation and validation of credit transactions against these quality standards. Potential limitations include issues with documentation accuracy and completeness, delays in the validation and cancelation processes, and the consistency in applying and interpreting the quality standards across different projects and registries.

The metric concerning total carbon credits to be canceled in the future is determined by identifying the number of carbon credits allocated for cancelation based on projected future needs. Potential limitations include uncertainties regarding future regulatory changes, market volatility affecting the availability and cost of carbon credits, and the accuracy of estimating future needs.

The metrics are validated by an external body other than the assurance provider in line with the respective recognized quality standards.

<!-- Report Page 129 -->
### [DR E1-8] Internal Carbon Pricing

[DR E1-8 63 a,b; AR 65] OMV Petrom applies internal carbon pricing for investment decisions across all business segments. In the base case, the costs of CO2 emissions are included wherever carbon pricing schemes are in place. Additionally, a stress test based on a "net zero emissions by 2050" scenario is conducted, applying shadow prices to all of OMV Petrom's Scope 1 emissions, except for certain activities in OMV Petrom, which fall outside of the EU ETS until 2030.

From 2030 onward, the internal carbon prices are applied to 100% of Scope 1 emissions.

The internal carbon prices applied are consistent with the carbon prices included in the mid-term planning (MTP). Base case carbon prices are informed by the IEA's Announced Pledges Scenario (APS) and other external and internal market analyses, while the "net zero emissions by 2050" case prices are largely based on the IEA's Net Zero Emissions by 2050 (NZE) scenario.

[DR E1-8 63 d] In 2024, the gross Scope 1 GHG emission volumes covered by the pricing schemes amounted to 2.9 mn t CO2 eq, representing 77% of total 3.8 mn Scope 1 GHG emissions.

In 2024, the gross Scope 3 GHG emission volumes were not covered by pricing schemes.

## Pollution

Liquid hydrocarbon spills remain a significant environmental concern, with potential breaches of environmental regulations, health and safety issues, and environmental damage. The soils contamination, a common impact in the oil and gas industry, mainly results from accidental spills of hydrocarbon products, leaks from pipelines or contaminated effluent discharge. Pollutants consist mainly of hydrocarbon products (spills).

Air pollutants other than GHG emissions generated from our supplier's activities, our operations and the use of OMV Petrom's products negatively impact air quality and may have adverse effects on human health, biodiversity, and the ecosystems. The pollutants associated with our operations

are attributed to various activities such as flaring, venting, fuel combustion for powering machinery, and transportation. In addition to these activities, air emissions can result from evaporation losses, fugitive emissions from tanks, equipment leakages and failures, and process safety incidents.

Health, Safety, Security, and protection of the Environment are embedded in all activities, according to OMV Petrom Group HSSE Policy and also in line with our HSSE Strategy
<!-- Report Page 130 -->
2030. We aim at preventing spills and appropriately
managing the response in case of occurrence and minimizing our non GHG emissions.

## Impact, risk and opportunity management [E2-1] Policies

[E2-1.14] OMV Petrom has the following policies in place to manage its material impacts related to pollution.

Code of Conduct

[E2-1.15a, MDR-P-65a] We are committed to implementing loss prevention and control measures to avoid soil pollution, following regulatory frameworks and industry best practices. This includes promptly cleaning up any spills and conducting immediate assessments to prevent recurrence. Additionally, we aim to improve air quality around operational sites.

In 2024, we updated our Code of Conduct and reinforced our focus on environmental protection. We are committed to implementing loss prevention and control measures to avoid soil pollution, by following regulatory frameworks and industry best practice. Any spill shall be cleaned up promptly and immediate assessment shall be done to prevent further occurrence.

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter.

### Human Rights Policy

<!-- Report Page 131 -->
[MDR-P-65a] Our commitments under this policy include enhancing carbon efficiency, supporting the energy transition, and achieving net-zero operations by 2050. We also prioritize responsible management of natural resources and aim to minimize environmental impacts through the reduction of soil pollution and emissions.

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter.

### OMV Petrom HSSE Directive and the Major Accidents Prevention Policy, as Annex

[MDR-P-65a] The OMV Petrom HSSE Directive mandates that all projects are managed with comprehensive consideration of HSSE aspects throughout their entire lifecycle, from design to execution. This includes engineering facilities to meet relevant codes for safe and environmentally sound operations, conducting HSSE risk assessments, performing health, environmental, and social impact assessments, and ensuring technical integrity and quality assurance during facility development. Through the HSSE Policy, as part of the Directive, we minimize our impact on the environment by minimizing pollution.

The Major Accident Prevention Policy is an Annex to the HSSE Directive and sets out the principles for controlling the risk of a major accident by requiring the following actions: identification and assessment of all major hazards, establishing risk controls to prevent major accidents from occurring and feasible and viable mitigation measures to minimize the consequences of major accidents to people and the environment, independent of the likelihood of occurrence. Measures are put in place to control and limit the impact on people and the environment if such events do occur.

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter

### Environmental Management standard

[MDR-P-65a] Our Environmental Management standard aims to minimize environmental impacts from air emissions, and spill management across OMV Petrom operations. Air emissions, both from point sources and fugitive emissions, are systematically monitored or estimated, controlled, and minimized to mitigate potential human health effects and harm to the environment, following the precautionary principle and adhering to international best industry practices and/or Best Available Techniques (BAT). New developments, upgrades, or major changes in existing operations incorporate emissions reduction considerations at all decision-making phases through value improvement practices and engineering. Furthermore, we monitor and control air emissions using best practices. Spill prevention and control measures include hazard identification, risk assessment, and emergency response plans, with any spills managed, cleaned, and reported internally

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter.

### HSSE Risk Management Standard

[MDR-P-65a] The HSSE Risk Management Standard sets the framework for identifying, assessing, controlling, documenting, and communicating HSSE risks, which includes risks related to the environment. Under this framework, risks for various environmental aspects are identified, including risks to air and soil. The standard also sets out minimum requirements for managing the environmental risks. It allows OMV Petrom to seize opportunities while prioritizing the protection of the environment.

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter.

### Incident, Emergency, Crisis and Business Continuity Management (Resilience)

[MDR-P-65a, E2-1.15c] The Incident, Emergency, Crisis, and Business Continuity Management (Resilience) Policy provides a structured framework for a rapid and coordinated response to incidents, emergencies, and crises. The policy prioritizes life protection, environmental preservation, asset security, and minimization of business interruptions. Each site has established incident response and management teams to handle emergencies, coordinate responses, and support local authorities.

[MDR-P 65b] The policy applies to OMV Petrom group and all its fully consolidated subsidiaries.

[MDR-P 65e] This standard applies to OMV Petrom's response arrangements for incidents, emergencies, and crises involving people, information, assets, or reputation, ensuring continuity where OMV Petrom holds legal, ethical, community, or business responsibilities.

[MDR P 65c] The Executive Board is accountable for implementing this policy, whereas clear responsibilities are laid down on functional level.

### Management of Change standard

[MDR-65P-a-c, e, f] The Management of Change standard ensures that any modifications to processes, systems, designs or organizational structures are carefully evaluated for potential new hazards or increased risks. Each change undergoes a structured assessment to establish risk controls and requires approval prior to implementation. This policy applies to all changes within OMV Petrom Group that could impact health and safety, the environment, quality, financial and asset integrity, reputation, or compliance, extending to contractors and leased personnel working on OMV's behalf. Accountability for the policy's implementation rests with the Executive Board. All internal regulations undergo an internal consultation process prior to their approvals/release. The policy is published on our internal regulations platform and is available to all stakeholders involved in the Management of Change process, including employees, suppliers, and contractors, who are kept informed of relevant changes and their progress via the OMV Group Management System, applicable to OMV Petrom Group as well.
