In addition, OMV Petrom implements continuous measures that contribute to the mitigation of negative impacts regarding pollution.Such measures are:

### Risk Management

[E2-2.18a] The risk management framework of OMV Petrom is aligned to the OMV Group framework, which manages safety and environmental risks to protect people, the environment, and company assets while ensuring business integrity. Pollution risks are central to our corporate and HSSE framework. We address asset integrity risks that could lead to pollution through our Integrated Risk Register (IGRR). Our asset integrity programs focus on identifying and managing risks of pollution, including crude oil spills. When a pollution risk is identified, we implement response measures in line with Group standards and local legal requirements. Such measures are:

### Monitoring

[E2-2.18a] We monitor emissions of pollutants in compliance with European and national regulations. If emissions exceed legal or permit limits, we take corrective measures.

<!-- Report Page 133 -->
Prevention and Treatment

[E2-2.18a, MDR-A68-a-c,e] The LDAR (Leak Detection and Repair) program focuses on reducing air pollution by minimizing losses of containment and the associated air emissions. The program aims to prevent and control air pollution through proactive measures to detect and address leaks. This initiative contributes to environmental policy

objectives by ensuring the prevention and control of air pollution and supporting sustainable operations

[MDR-A68-a-c,e)] Tanks modernization Program in Refinery Program targets reducing volatile organic compound (VOC) emissions to the air by upgrading tank systems to align with modern standards. This initiative supports environmental policy objectives by preventing pollution, minimizing environmental impacts, and ensuring the efficient use of assets

[MDR-A68-a-c,e)] Pipeline integrity program focuses on technical improvements for the integrity of onshore conventional oil and gas production, aiming to prevent the loss of containment and establish barriers against pollution. This program addresses risks to soil, air, and potentially water by implementing specific projects, such as risk studies, corrosion management plans, pipeline and equipment replacements, capital repairs, reconstructions, and technological flow improvements. It contributes to environmental policy objectives by preventing pollution, minimizing environmental impacts, and transitioning toward safer and less polluting operational practices. Additional benefits include emissions reduction and improved flow measurements.

[MDR-A68-a-c,d] Surface facilities optimizations program focuses on technical upgrades and optimizations of facilities for onshore conventional oil and gas production to prevent loss of containment and create a more efficient operating regime. These improvements ensure barriers against pollution by controlling, reducing, or enhancing/replacing equipment to prevent pollution, particularly to air and soil, while also lowering utilities and resource consumption. The initiative supports environmental policy objectives by preventing pollution and minimizing environmental impacts. The program is set to commence in 2025.

#### Metrics and targets [DR E2-3] Targets related to pollution

[ESRS 2 MDR-T.81] Pollution is primarily addressed in our HSSE Policy under our commitment to protect the environment by implementing our vision "Committed to Zero Harm - Protect People, Environment and Assets", with specific focus on the area of minimizing our impact on the environment by minimizing environmental factors pollution.

In this sense, as we have not set specific targets for pollution, we track the effectiveness in this focus area by monitoring our performance using industry specific metrics for pollution monitoring, as required by legislation and described below in the metrics section.

We evaluate our progress on an yearly basis, by assessing the evolution of these metrics and analyzing any abnormalities in the values recorded. This practice enables us to track the effectiveness of our actions for minimizing our environmental impact and to develop new actions where the need arises. While minimization of our environmental impact is a complex and multifactorial topic, it is directly impacted and supported by other actions developed to reach the specific targets our organization has set up on the topics of process safety, energy and GHG emissions reductions.

<!-- Report Page 134 -->

[ESRS 2 MDR-M.77 (a-c)] Spills occurred: Total Number of hydrocarbon Spills refers to the total number of spills registered and documented in the reporting system within reporting boundaries for the reporting year.

Of which major (i.e., severity levels 3 to 5): OMV Petrom incident classification system consists of five severity levels, with severity increasing from level 1 to level 5. A level 3 incident is defined as Medium environmental damage within a large area outside the boundaries for which actions for remediation / restoration are required

Spills volume released: Spills related data is gathered in the company's reporting system on a continuous basis. The

measurement unit for the volume released is the liter. Depending on the type and severity of spill and data availability, there are different methods employed to determine the spill volume. For larger volumes of spilled material, process data are used to determine the spill volume (e.g. tank volumes and levels, flow-measurements and alike). For smaller volumes of spilled material, the volume of excavated soil and the specific hydrocarbon loading of the soil is used. For very small volumes of spilled material, that don't require soil recovery, estimates will be employed.

## Water

Water is an integral part of our operations. Freshwater is withdrawn from all types of sources (groundwater, surface water and public network) and is used in all business segments (E&P, R&M, G&P), mainly for technological processes including: cooling, steam generation, drilling and injection (specific for E&P). Therefore, water is utilised in the entire flow, from drilling to final products distribution and electricity production.

All resulted water effluents are subject to treatment prior discharge into public sewage or surface water bodies.

Produced water is a process by-product generated from oil and gas extraction process and is managed by injection (>94% reinjection in hydrocarbon reservoirs) or treated and discharged (<6%).

As such, the impact of OMV Petrom, across its operations, including E&P, R&M, and G&P, consists in withdrawal and consumption of high volumes of freshwater, with the potential to impact both communities and other local businesses in terms of water resources.

# Impact, risk and opportunity management [DR E3-1] Policies

[E3-1.11] OMV Petrom has the following policies in place to manage its material impact related to water:

Code of Conduct

[MDR-P 65-a] [E3-1.AR 17, e, E3-1-12 a.i., E3-1-12.c] According to the Code of Conduct, we are reducing the water intensity of our operations. In 2024, we updated the Code of Conduct to extend our commitment regarding water use. We respect water as a precious limited resource and focus on its sustainable and efficient use. We respect the right to water of our stakeholders, especially local communities, and minimize the impact of water use (for instance by reducing freshwater withdrawals, especially in water-scarce areas) and discharge (for instance by improving (waste) water quality).

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter.

## Human Rights Policy

[MDR-P 65-a]The Human Rights Policy emphasizes our commitment to sustainability by minimizing environmental impacts through prevention of water and soil pollution, reduction of emissions, efficient use of natural resources, and protection of biodiversity, contributing to a sustainable world.

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter.

OMV Petrom Group HSSE Policy

[MDR-P 65-a] Through the OMV Petrom Group HSSE Policy, which is an Annex of the HSSE Directive, we commit to minimize environmental impacts by reducing pollution, lowering greenhouse gas emissions, efficiently using water

and natural resources, protecting biodiversity, and rehabilitating sites after closure.

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, please refer to Climate change chapter.

Environmental Management standard

[MDR-P 65-a, E3-1-12a.i] This regulation further defines the minimum requirements for environmental management in OMV Petrom Group, establishing the environmental risk assessment, minimum environmental performance requirements for water management and discharges. It mandates that all OMV Petrom activities use water efficiently and minimize impacts on the environment and local communities. Pollution prevention and impact minimization at the source are central to our water management approach.

All operational sites assess water-related risks and opportunities at a river-basin scale, maintain a detailed water balance account and develop management plans to address identified material risks. All operational sites have to create, implement, and maintain a water management plan that includes scope and objectives, applicable legislation, water sources and discharges, water mapping and balance, transport and treatment systems, significant risks and mitigation measures and water conservation and efficiency measures. Direct discharge of untreated wastewater onto land, wetlands, or other water bodies is prohibited, and no discharge may alter or reduce the value of the receiving environment. Discharge quality is managed to meet legal permit limits and mitigate potential impacts on the environment and local communities. Systematic monitoring ensures compliance and appropriate management of environmental impacts.

[E3-1-12.a.ii, E3-1-12.a.iii] Environmental Management Standard requires environmental impact assessments (EIAs) for all projects to avoid, mitigate, and manage potential impacts on water. These assessments address environmental risks, focusing on preserving water quality and preventing water stress, with internal processes ensuring compliance and asset adherence to legal requirements.

[E3-1.12.b, E3-1-12.c] OMV Petrom integrates water management into HSSE policies across the business lifecycle, including projects, procurement, and contracts. Environmental impacts, including those related to water, are assessed in each project through Environmental Risk and Impact studies. Standards for Supplier Performance and Contractor Management further reinforce water and

environmental sustainability across the supply chain and contract lifecycle.

[E3-1.14] OMV Petrom has not adopted any specific policies or practices related to sustainable oceans and seas.

<!-- Report Page 137 -->
[DR E3-2] Actions

[E3-2.17] In recent years, we have implemented several Best Available Technology (BAT) measures to minimize freshwater withdrawal and sustainably treat water. These include reducing operational complexity, increasing water recirculation (e.g., at Brazi power plant), using desalinated seawater instead of freshwater (e.g., Petromar), installing recirculating cooling systems, and using alternative cooling agents (air or glycol) instead of water, upgrade and continuous maintenance of equipment to reduce water loss and optimization of pipeline routes for water supply.

In 2024, in the Exploration & Production segment, we have continued our technical improvements for integrity and optimizations of our facilities including water systems thus preventing losses and generating more efficient operating regime. This systematic approach will be continued in the future as part of our actions to address efficient water management.

Various projects and measures contribute to reduce the use and supply of water in our operations. In 2024, specific projects were conducted and finalized:

[MDR-A 68-a, b, c] Modernization of technological water system in Asset Muntenia to ensure proper supply with freshwater.

[MDR-A 68-a, b, c] Rehabilitation of Compressors Station Bustuchin in Asset Oltenia, by replacing of existing cooling tower system with a modular new equipment having high efficiency

[MDR-A 68-a, b, c] Retrofit of cooling system at CHP unit Oprisenesti in Asset Moldova

Additionally, in line also with the requirements of the environmental policies, water management plans which include all our sites are prepared and updated on annual basis, aiming at improving the water use efficiency.

[ESRS 2 MDR-A 69] No significant CAPEX was required for the implementation of the actions related to water. Consequently, this topic is not reflected in the financial statement.

[E3-2.19] Overall water stress assessments are conducted periodically. In order to identify operations in areas affected by water scarcity and water stress, OMV Petrom uses international tools and indexes such as the Verisk Maplecroft's "Water Stress Index", as well as its own assessments as required. So far, no high water stress areas were identified for OMV Petrom operations.

## Metrics and targets [DR E3-3] Targets related to water

[ESRS 2-72] We have been monitoring our ambition established in our Code of Conduct, to reduce the freshwater use, through dedicated metric freshwater intensity. In order to set up relevant water-related targets, we are planning to expand our water risk analysis in order to have a solid analysis as a base for development.

[ESRS 2.81] While specific targets for water were not set, OMV Petrom ambition is to efficiently reduce freshwater

withdrawal and enhance the quality of wastewater in traditional businesses.

We are committed to treat water as a valuable, limited resource and to use it sustainably and efficiently. We respect the right to water of our stakeholders, especially local communities, and strive to reduce the impact of our water use and discharge. To measure our progress, we track the year-on-year reduction in freshwater withdrawal within our operations, as well as internally assess our overall freshwater intensity against industry benchmarks (eg. IOGP, Refinery BREF, etc).

Water withdrawn (cubic meters) is the sum of all water drawn into boundaries of the undertaking from all sources for any use over the course of reporting period, encompassing the following sources: surface water, groundwater, seawater, public supply systems and produced water resulted from hydrocarbon extraction process.

Water recycled and reused (cubic meters) is calculated based on the total volume of water recirculated in installations (eg. cooling systems); recirculated water is calculated from pump operating hours (hours/year) x pump flow rate (cubic meters /hour) = cubic meters /year volumes of water recirculated (cubic meters)

Water discharged (cubic meters) is the sum of effluents and other water leaving the boundaries of the organization and

released to: surface water or to third parties over the course of reporting period, measured and calculated.

[MDR-M 77a] Water consumption (cubic meters) is defined as the amount of water drawn within boundaries of undertaking and not discharged back to the water environment or to a third party over the course of reporting period. Total water consumption is calculated as the difference between total water withdrawn and total water discharged.

[MDR-M 77b, E3-4.28e] ]. Metering devices used to measure water flows have EEC label, which is an indicator of external validation. However, the data reading and collection process for offline meters is managed internally and does not involve an independent body.

[MDR-M.77a, b] Water intensity is calculated as total water consumption in operations in cubic meters per million EUR net revenue. Total water consumption includes produced water.

Water intensity = Total water consumption (cubic meters) / Net revenue (million EUR).

The Net revenue used to calculate the water intensity is disclosed in the consolidated financial statements, note 19 "Sales revenues", line "Total sales revenues", using the average EUR FX rate from Note 6.

# Biodiversity and ecosystems

We acknowledge that some of our operations are located within one-kilometer buffer to Natural Protected Areas (NPAs) and can have impacts on biodiversity. The specific impacts of our industry include habitat loss and alteration through land use for exploration, production, disposal of drilling wastes and associated by-products, and

decommissioning of onshore/ offshore wells. In the long term, oil and gas industry specific activities can lead to indirect and cumulative impacts on biodiversity meaning limitations in the availability, accessibility, or quality of natural resources, and this, in turn, may adversely impact the well-being and livelihoods of local communities.

## Strategy

### [DR E4-1] Transition plan and consideration of biodiversity and ecosystems in strategy and business model

[E4-1.11] We are aware that our operations have the potential to generate impacts on biodiversity.

<!-- Report Page 141 -->
Our approach to biodiversity management is in line with our HSSE Strategy 2030, launched in the last quarter of 2023. The strategy sets a goal to protect people and nature and respect the planetary boundaries. In this direction, the specific biodiversity aim is for net positive impact in new operated projects in sensitive areas. We also commit to restore the used land and bring it back to its original condition at the end of our operations, as outlined in our HSSE Policy and in accordance with have the legal obligations.

[E4-1.13a-f] An understanding of our biodiversity impacts, dependencies, risks, and opportunities, as expected to be revealed through our TNFD (Taskforce for Nature Financial Disclosure) LEAP (Locate, Evaluate, Assess, Prepare) assessment, is essential to inform a resilience analysis of

our business model and strategy. As the assessment is still in progress, a resilience analysis has not been performed.

### [DR SBM 3] Material impacts, risks and opportunities and their interaction with strategy and business model

[4-SBM 3.16] A geospatial analysis revealed that several OMV Petrom sites are located within or near biodiversity areas. For a clear materiality of specific locations, recently we have started a comprehensive evaluation and assessment over the operational activities, following a LEAP assessment guided by TNFD and considering local and national public acknowledgement of the state of nature for each regional area. In order to define a list of potential material sites, we have to finalize the assessment.

[E4-SBM 3.16a i] OMV Petrom recognizes that its operations, particularly those carried out in locations near NPAs (National Protected Areas), as revealed by the geospatial analysis, have the potential to generate impacts on biodiversity. Our operations which could trigger biodiversity related impacts and risks are falling under the major streams in the oil & gas industry. These refer mainly to operations such as exploration activities, including geophysical methods, drilling new wells, oil & gas extraction

and primary treatment of oil, through decommissioning of production infrastructure and sites remediation. Impacts are indirect and cumulative and can include habitat loss or fragmentation and alteration through land use for exploration, production, disposing of drilling and associated wastes, also collaterally coming from pollution of air, soil (degradation).

[E4-SBM 3.16a.ii] Based on the TNFD LEAP assessment initiated in the last quarter of 2023, we will be able to determine site-level impacts and dependencies, by also considering the ecological status of these sites. After working with three pilot sites in the first phase of the project, we plan to continue the roll-out of this assessment to all relevant operational sites (excluding filling stations).

[E4-SBM 3.16a.iii] We operate inside various types of NPAs such as National Protected Areas, Natura 2000 and Key Biodiversity Areas (KBA). The Evaluate and Assess steps of the LEAP assessment need to be completed for all sites in Romania to be able to provide a list of material sites that impact biodiversity sensitive areas. As the process of completing the evaluation is still ongoing, we can provide only an estimated surface of sites in or near biodiversitysensitive areas, regardless of the potential impact on these areas. For more details, refer to E4-5 section in this chapter.

[E4-SBM 3.16b] As result of the TNFD LEAP assessment project, a complete list of material sites which could be associated with negative impacts with regards to land degradation, desertification or soil sealing will be available. For the existing sites assessed so far, we have not identified material negative impacts with regards to land degradation, desertification or soil sealing.

[E4-SBM 3.16c] We currently have not identified any impact of our operations that would affect threatened species, based on the TNFD LEAP assessment performed so far.

#### Impact, risk and opportunity management [DR E4-2] Policies related to biodiversity and ecosystems

[E4-2.22, MDRP-A] Potential impacts on biodiversity are managed according to the policies below:

##### Code of Conduct

[MDR-P 65a] At OMV Petrom Group, we are committed to ensuring sustainable and responsible behaviour towards the environment, and society, recognizing the significant impact of our operations and value chain. We are dedicated to conducting our business responsibly, with respect for the environment and a focus on adding value to the communities where we operate. Guided by high ethical standards, we apply these principles in all aspects of our operations and expect the same from our collaborators.

In December 2024, we updated our Code of Conduct to underpin our commitment to preserving and restoring biodiversity and land and marine ecosystems in alignment with the post-2020 Global Biodiversity Framework (GBF). We apply the mitigation hierarchy, with priority given to avoidance and minimization over restoration and offsetting
<!-- Report Page 142 -->

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, refer to the Climate change chapter.

#### Environmental Management Standard

[MDR-P 65a] The standard outlines the criteria for an environmental management system (EMS), emphasizing compliance with environmental laws, minimizing environmental impacts, and using best practices and technology. In terms of biodiversity, the standard mandates all OMV Petrom Group businesses and activities to minimize disturbance and impacts on local plant and wildlife communities. Biodiversity and ecosystem services (BES) screenings are to be carried out within the EMS framework to identify potential threats to species, ecosystems, and legally protected or internationally recognized areas. If significant biodiversity impacts are observed or predicted, a biodiversity action plan should be incorporated into the Environmental Management Plan. This plan should cover the social, regulatory, and ecosystem context, partnerships with external stakeholders, biodiversity baseline survey and impact assessment, and mitigation and biodiversity conservation measures and monitoring. The standard also includes a hierarchy for biodiversity protection: avoid, minimize, restore, and offset impacts.

[MDR_P 65 b-f] For more details, refer to the Climate change chapter.

[E4-2.23a, AR 4a, i-v] The Environmental Management Standard regulates the necessity to minimize disturbance and impacts on local wildlife communities, which could involve re-routing pipelines and re-sitting fixed facilities to avoid impacts, and erosion control to minimize impacts. This

is especially relevant for impacts related to land-use change, pollution, or direct exploitation, even if these direct impact drivers are not explicitly mentioned in the standard. Impacts relating to invasive alien species are not specifically covered by the standard. BES must be considered in the managed activities, either new entries with specific evaluations or in operations cycle through compliance to environmental permits. Water management plans are in place to optimize the use of water resources, according to the Environmental Management Standard. Environmental Risk Assessments are a recurrent revision task, according to the same Standard. Emergency Response plans are in place according to the Water Management permitting framework as well as according to Incident, Emergency, Crisis and Business Continuity Management (Resilience) Standard.

[E4-2.23a AR 4b-d] Potential impacts on the state of species, the extent and condition of ecosystems including through land degradation, desertification and dependencies on ecosystem services have not resulted as material. However, the EMS requires BES screenings to identify the potential presence of nationally or globally threatened species and to describe and analyze observed or predicted impacts on biodiversity within the environmental impact assessment.

The EMS also includes, as part of the hierarchy for biodiversity protection, measures to minimize physical footprints and control erosion, restore sites through rehabilitation, and offset residual impacts through biodiversity compensations. This addresses the extent and condition of ecosystems, including land degradation and desertification. Additionally, it requires the description and analysis of impacts on ecosystem services within the environmental impact assessment and incorporates biodiversity action planning into the Environmental Management Plan, which includes mitigation and conservation measures to protect ecosystem services.

[E4-2.23b] According to the EMS, when significant impacts have been observed or predicted, defined as recognizing effects that severely affect entire habitats or significant portions thereof, impairing their viability or function, the mitigation hierarchy is followed, and action planning prioritizes the avoidance and minimization of impacts rather than restoration and offsetting.

[E4-2.23c] Our EMS requires the assessment of environmental impacts and risks, as well as adherence to environmental performance requirements concerning energy use, emissions into the atmosphere, water use and discharge, raw materials usage, waste management, hazardous substance handling, and biodiversity and

ecosystem protection. However, dependencies and the physical and transition risks and opportunities on biodiversity and ecosystems services are not reflected specifically in our policies.

[E4-2.23d] According to the updated Code of Conduct, OMV Petrom Group cooperates with business partners to ensure that the impacts of our value chain do not compromise this commitment to preserving and restoring biodiversity and land and marine ecosystems in alignment with the post-2020 GBF. This commitment is reinforced by adherence to contractual conditions that reference all specific HSSE documents. Contracts are considered binding only through the enforcement of HSSE regulations, which ensures that all parties are held accountable for upholding these standards.

[E4-2.23e] In our business operations, we do not produce, source, or consume materials from ecosystems. However, as we further develop our low carbon business and engage in activities that require managing ecosystems, OMV Petrom is committed to reducing its environmental footprint and supporting a sustainable energy future while maintaining or enhancing biodiversity through regular monitoring and reporting as per legal requirements.

[E4-2.23f] Prior to new operational activities or new country entries, environmental risk assessments are performed. This includes evaluations of local legislation and the potential impact of our activities on sensitive and protected areas, as well as the effects on endangered species. When required by the permitting process, Environmental and Social Impact Assessments (ESIAs) for new developments are submitted to the local regulator or lender (whichever is applicable) for review, public disclosure, and approval. An Environmental and Social Management Plan is prepared to include preventive and mitigation measures as well as the monitoring program. Monitoring is carried out during the operational phase in accordance with the requirements set forth in the ESM Plan or as mandated by the authorities, which are integral to the authorization permits.

[E4-2.24a-d] OMV Petrom has not yet adopted a biodiversity and ecosystem protection policy covering operational sites owned, leased, or managed in or near biodiversity sensitive areas. However, our EMS and Code of Conduct apply to all our operational sites, regardless of their proximity to biodiversity sensitive areas. Policies explicitly covering sustainable land or sustainable seas practices, or that addresses deforestation have not been developed. Based on the results of the LEAP assessment on our sites, we will assess whether and how to incorporate this detailed information into our policies.

<!-- Report Page 143 -->
#### [DR E4-3] Actions

[E4-3.25, ESRS 2 MDR-A 68] Our Environmental Management Standard defines measures to manage the negative impact on biodiversity and ecosystems from various impact drivers, such as GHG emissions, land use change, water and resource use, pollutant release, and disturbances. Key measures include site-level assessments and biodiversity action plans. These measures represent the starting point for future improvements and upgrades

Evaluation and Assessment of Biodiversity aspects following TNFD guidance

[MDR-A68 a, b, c, e] The effectiveness of our biodiversity policies and actions is tracked through the progress of our LEAP assessment, which currently entails the evaluation and assessment of pilot locations within OMV Petrom as per LEAP approach guided by TNFD. This assessment supports our ambition of preserving and restoring biodiversity and land and marine ecosystems.

LEAP is used to identify and assess nature-related issues through four distinct phases.

In the Locate step, the OMV Petrom sites are subjected to geospatial analysis to prioritize sites based on ecosystem integrity and biodiversity importance. Various biodiversity data layers provided by IBAT (layers on protected areas, key biodiversity area and IUCN red listed species), as well as freely available layers (ESRI Land cover), mean species abundance and water stress, are applied. The results of the Locate phase were also used to select three pilot sites across Romania.

In the Evaluate step, a corporate impact and dependency register was built by matching OMV Petrom's activities with data from the ENCORE tool and TNFD sector guidance. This corporate register is used as a starting point for the site level assessment for the pilot sites where the impact drivers are correlated with nature impacts on species, habitats, and ecosystems. To rate the site level impacts, the magnitude of effect for nature impacts and receptor sensitivity need to be scored. In a similar way dependencies are evaluated.

The results of the Evaluate phase are then used as an input for the Assess phase, where risks and opportunities are analyzed. In 2024, the three pilot sites in E&P underwent an evaluation and assessment approach to identify the biodiversity materiality degree and to appreciate the level of potential risks for further deeper understanding on how all activities could trigger biodiversity loss. These pilot evaluations are essential to create an accurate view of the

impact and establish a baseline for biodiversity management planning, which will be further refined.

In the Prepare phase, we will focus on getting ready for disclosure and conducting preparatory work related to metrics and targets, an updated biodiversity policy and a roadmap for further roll out.

Our EMS provides measures for BES screenings, which are expected to guide us is developing Biodiversity Action Plans (BAPs) for the actual operations and projects with significant impacts or risks.

[E4-3.28b] The mitigation hierarchy is a key principle in the development of biodiversity action plans and is also set out in our Environmental Management Standard. Biodiversity offsets represent the last stage of the mitigation hierarchy for biodiversity related impacts and shall only be used when all other options do not achieve the desired results. In 2024, OMV Petrom did not make use of biodiversity offsets

[E4-3.28c] Since the LEAP assessment was still in progress, action plans have not yet been defined. During the definition phase, relevant local knowledge, as well as potential naturebased solutions, will be explored and assessed

##### Metrics and targets [DR E4-4] Targets related to biodiversity and ecosystems

[ESRS 2 MDR-T.72, MDR-T.81b] In the updated Code of Conduct, OMV Petrom has included an ambition to preserving and restoring biodiversity and land and marine ecosystems in alignment with the post-2020 Global Biodiversity Framework (GBF), prioritizing avoidance and minimization over restoration and offsetting through the mitigation hierarchy. A more systematic approach based on international frameworks, such as TNFD, is necessary in order to set up relevant targets. In this sense, our LEAP assessment (please see section Actions) will form the foundation for developing ESRS-aligned targets, which, at the moment of publishing the Sustainability Statement, were not defined. Currently, the effectiveness of biodiversity policies and actions is tracked through regular monitoring of sites overlapping with biodiversity sensitive areas and the progress of the LEAP assessment.

### [DR E4-5] Impact metrics related to biodiversity and ecosystems change

As part of our LEAP assessment, we have begun to evaluate the biodiversity and ecosystems impacts and dependencies of these sites on sensitive areas. However, a

comprehensive understanding of the materiality of impacts and dependencies at the site level is not yet available. Therefore, the following table does not report on the area of material sites in or near biodiversity-sensitive areas but provides an overview of the OMV Petrom sites near

biodiversity-sensitive areas, regardless of the impact on the area. The metrics below are disclosed voluntarily as they contribute to our overall ambition of preserving and restoring biodiversity and ecosystems.

[ESRS 2 MDR-M.75-77] We updated our disclosures from the previous reporting period to align with the requirements of the ESRS, including the incorporation of key biodiversity areas, which were not previously addressed. The area of sites located in or near biodiversity-sensitive areas in aggregated form is defined as the total area of OMV Petrom, the sites (in hectars) that are located within 1 km distance biodiversity sensitive areas. The metric is calculated using geospatial analysis. Based on GIS coordinates of our operational assets, open-source data sets were accessed and overlapped to obtain relevant information related to natural protected areas and key biodiversity areas. A categorization of sensitive areas was obtained looking at operational locations clustered into groups based on region for E&P business.

The metric does not account for the area of overlap between OMV Petrom sites and the sensitive areas enlarged by the 1 km buffer zone. In contrast, if any part of the site falls within the 1 km buffer zone, the entire site is considered. A granular analysis based on individual wells and facilities was performed. Since all wells and many facilities are entered as data point (i.e. without area), in our GIS system, proxy polygons with average areas had to be created and used in

the geospatial analysis. For wells, an average proxy area of 900 m2 and for facilities an average area of 5000 m2 was used in the analysis.

Filling stations and pipelines are excluded from the analysis, and the same approach was considered for "administrative" facilities (e.g. office buildings outside of industrial sites facilities) which do not impact on biodiversity and ecosystems.

Assumptions and limitations on this metric are mainly related to the use of proxy area for OMV Petrom facilities and wells in the E&P Division. The metric is not species or ecosystem and does not integrate ecological threshold. Primary data (OMV Petrom operations) and public available data on biodiversity sensitive areas is used in calculating the metric. The used datasets are subject to accuracy degree depending on their last year of update and it was not verified externally by a specialized professional organization. During evaluations of pilot sites, the need emerged to check the status of sensitive areas at local authorities, in order to fine tune the results and improve the accuracy of intersected operational sites / clusters with the areas identified and managed by governmental bodies.

# Process safety

Process Safety Management (PSM) remains a crucial focus for the company, given it is both a moral and a business imperative. We understand that a process safety incident might lead not just to the loss of life, but could also substantially impact business value.

PSM is characterized by the active detection, analysis, evaluation, and mitigation of hazardous substance releases

and process incidents that could possibly ensue from failures in procedures, equipment, or process technology.

In summary, PSM is fundamental to our company's moral and business obligations. We recognize the negative impact of a potential major accident, and we are committed to mitigating the risks through effective prevention measures.

## Impact, risk and opportunity management [MDR-P] Policies

[ESRS 2-MDR-P.65] The specific policies linked to Process Safety are as follows:

### Major Accident Prevention Policy

[MDR-P-a] This policy outlines OMV Petrom's overall aims and arrangements for controlling major accident risks in its operations, detailing how these objectives are achieved and the specific measures implemented across the organization. Central to this policy is OMV Petrom's commitment to cultivating a strong safety culture that ensures a high likelihood of safe, continuous operations.

[MDR-P b,c,d,e,f] For more details, please see the Climate change chapter.

## HSSE Risk Management Standard

[MDR-P-a] The HSSE Risk Management Standard establishes the framework and minimum requirements for managing HSSE risks at OMV Petrom. Effective HSSE risk management supports decision-making, is integral to handling changes (including temporary ones) and ensures that every decision considers risk. Managing HSSE risks enables OMV Petrom to pursue opportunities while prioritizing the protection of people, the environment, and assets to maintain business integrity. This standard's objectives include defining a process for identifying, assessing, controlling, documenting, and communicating HSSE risks.

[MDR-P b,c,d,e,f] For more details, please see the Climate change chapter.

## Process Safety Management Standard

[MDR-P a, b, c, f] The Process Safety Management (PSM) Standard provides a framework for implementing and

maintaining effective process safety regulations, supporting the development, execution, and review of detailed standards and procedures governing process safety activities and systems. PSM is embedded within the overall Group HSSE management, employing uniform instructions, practices, and specifications to achieve safe and reliable production. Key components include organizational structure, resources, management processes, personnel and equipment performance, safety culture, and documented regulations and practices. This standard applies to OMV Petrom SA and its fully consolidated subsidiaries. Senior Management, including Managing Directors, General Managers, or Affiliate Directors, holds accountability for implementing the standard. Standard requirements are communicated through internal channels like the internal regulations' platform, with training provided upon major revisions. Additionally, requirements are included in the tendering process during the pre-award phase as part of Minimum HSSE Requirements and reinforced through Bridging Documents during Contract Execution.

## HSSE in Major Capital Projects Standard

[MDR-P-a, b, c, f] This procedure specifies required HSSE, process safety and technical safety related deliverables/activities in the phases of project life cycle. The regulation covers both 'greenfield' and 'brownfield' major capital projects within OMV Petrom and all its fully consolidated subsidiaries. The project owner is accountable for the implementation of the procedure. We communicate the procedure requirements through internal channels, such as our internal regulations platform, including training whenever a major revision of the procedure appears and during the tendering process in pre-awarding phase as part of Minimum HSSE Requirements and through Bridging Document during the Contract Execution phase.

<!-- Report Page 146 -->
# [MDR-A] Actions and resources

[ESRS 2-MDR-A.68] OMV Petrom has various actions in place to address material impacts related to Process Safety:

## Development of incident scenarios

Incident scenarios are developed and assessed for risks associated with the operational activity, in line with Enterprise Risk Management and HSSE Risk Management Standard. Mitigation measures are established and monitored. Information regarding major risks is kept in the group-wide risk database and all the others risks in the operational risk registers. This action applies to entity facilities/processes.

[MDR-A.68-a, b, c, d] Major risks and corresponding mitigation measures are documented, evaluated, and monitored in a Group-wide database called the Active Risk Management System (ARMS), which is updated biannually and involves senior management in reviewing top-priority risks. Risk registers are maintained in operational areas as required by the Risk Management Standard and are consistently updated at different organizational levels to keep risks controlled and reduce the likelihood and potential consequences of incidents. ARMS risks are revised twice a year, with local risk registers updated whenever working conditions change. Mitigation actions are closely tracked in both ARMS and local registers.

## Development and implementation of the Process Safety Roadmaps

[MDR-A68-a, b, c, d, e] The objective of this action is to define a clear sequence of activities Process Safety Information, Process Hazard Identification and Risk Assessment, and Risk Management Elements for enhancing safety across facilities. Actions taken include ensuring that all facilities have Roadmaps by the end of 2025 and completing all actions outlined in the Process Safety Roadmaps each year. Covering E&P and the Petrobrazi Refinery we monitor on quarterly basis the progress on roadmaps definition and actions implementation. In 2024, 65 roadmaps were developed and 467 actions were implemented.

## Improving quality of data in IGRR (Integrated Risk Register) by quarterly review

[MDR-A68-a, b, c, d, e] The Integrated Global Risk Register (IGRR) provides a semi-quantitative risk analysis, allowing for a Prioritization Score Assessment (PSA) of process safety actions, which assesses the risk reduction achieved by implementing each action relative to its effort. Applicable to E&P and the Petrobrazi Refinery, the IGRR is updated quarterly by local or site process safety leads. Regular reviews are conducted for the status and cost of critical actions, and the IGRR tracks items requiring temporary compensating measures. These quarterly updates include all High and Medium Risk actions arising from safety studies and audits.

Process safety risks assessed, mitigation measures follow-up through different tools and programs

[MDR-A68-a, b, c, d] In line with ensuring asset integrity, OMV Petrom implements Pipeline Integrity Management and Inspection Programs, Corrosion Management Plans in E&P, and the Asset Integrity Data Assistant (AIDA) in R&M.

Covering E&P and the Petrobrazi Refinery, this initiative tracks key indicators such as the number of inspections performed under the pipeline integrity program, the number of corrosion management plans developed in 2024, and the number of inspections in the Refinery. This action is monitored and updated annually. In 2024, 47 Corrosion Management Plans for pipelines, facilities, and projects were developed, and 134 inspections were performed for pipeline integrity. In addition, for assessing process safety risks, the PHA (Process Hazard Analysis) program continues through development of HAZOP (Hazards and Operability) studies. In E&P, in 2024 we performed 64 HAZOP (HAZards and OPerability) studies.

Knowledge and awareness related to process safety developed continuously

[MDR-A68-a, b, c, d] To enhance knowledge sharing, best practices and learning from events, OMV Petrom implements a series of actions across E&P and the Petrobrazi Refinery, including the distribution of Beacon alerts through dedicated channels (such as email, control room postings, and discussions in meetings), the participation of its specialists in OMV Group Process Safety Knowledge Exchange Sessions, and the allocation of Process Safety Computer-Based Training (CBT) modules to specific target groups. Key performance indicators include the number of Beacon alerts distributed, the number of Knowledge Exchange Sessions, and the percentage of planned personnel who completed training. In 2024, 12 Beacon alerts were distributed, process safety specialists participate in four Knowledge Exchange Sessions, and ~87 % of planned participants completed the dedicated training modules.

## PS incidents investigated and alerts and lessons learned developed and shared

[MDR-A68-a, b, c, d] To ensure incident prevention and continuous safety improvements, all Tier 1 and Tier 2 incidents within E&P and R&M are thoroughly investigated to determine direct, root, and systemic causes. Preventive and corrective actions are then implemented to minimize the likelihood of recurrence to as low as reasonably practicable (ALARP). Additionally, safety alerts and lessons learned are developed and distributed to prevent similar incidents based on these findings. These actions are maintained on a permanent basis, with metrics tracking the number of Tier 1 and Tier 2 incidents investigated, along with the number of alerts and lessons learned distributed.

Safety studies developed early in the project lifecycle and verified during Project HSSE Reviews or Independent Gate Reviews processes.

[MDR-A68-a, b, c, d] Process hazard identification and risk assessments are conducted throughout all phases of a facility's lifecycle, including concept, design, construction, operation, and decommissioning. The concept of Inherently Safe Design is adopted and implemented as an initial step during the conceptual and design phases to enhance safety from the outset. This action applies to E&P, R&M, and G&P and aligns with each project's schedule. Key performance indicators include the number of safety studies completed in various projects. In 2024 we performed 59 studies.

Process safety incidents may result in loss of containment. As such, other actions to prevent and, wherever applicable, mitigate the negative impact identified for the material topic Process Safety, largely contribute to the prevention and mitigation of the company's impacts and risks related to E2 Pollution, and viceversa. Please see Pollution chapter for more details. [ESRS 2 MDR-A.69].

# Metrics and targets [MDR-T] Targets related to process safety

<table>
<tr>
<th>Target</th>
<th>Progress 2024</th>
</tr>
<tr>
<!-- Report Page 147 -->
<td>Maintain leading position in Process Safety Event Rate</td>
<td>0.16</td>
</tr>
</table>

[MDR-T-a, b, c, e, f, h, j] The target is directly linked to OMV Petrom's HSSE policy, aligning with the ambition to become an industry leader in HSSE and guided by the vision "Committed to Zero Harm Protect People, Environment, and Assets". The scope of the target covers OMV Petrom Group and its fully consolidated subsidiaries. The target is set as a relative measure, using 2017 as the baseline year and

extending to 2030. The normalized rate of process safety events (PSER) is calculated jointly for Tier 1 and Tier 2 process safety events. Given the lack of a universally applicable normalization factor for process safety indicators based on facility configuration, worker exposure hours are used as a convenient and accessible factor, encompassing total hours worked by employees and contractors within the

reporting scope. E&P includes hours worked for operated assets, R&M includes all operations. Hours worked in Corporate Functions (e.g., General Management and Finance in Petrom) are excluded. Due to the typically low frequency of process safety events, care is advised when interpreting the PSER, as these rates are generally more statistically valid at an industry or company level. The PSER is calculated as PSER = (PSE Tier 1 + Tier 2) / work hours * 1,000,000. This target was established through a top-down

approach without stakeholder involvement and OMV Petrom benchmarks its performance against IOGP data.

[ESRS 2 MDR-M.77 a, b, c] Process Safety Events (PSEs) of Tier 1 and Tier 2 are evaluated on a periodical basis to maintain consistent insight into OMV Petrom's process safety performance. Each PSE is recorded and monitored in the Synergi reporting system. The metric measurement is not validated by an external body. PSEs are defined as unplanned or uncontrolled releases of any material, including non-toxic and non-flammable substances (e.g.,

steam, hot water, nitrogen, compressed CO, or compressed air) from a process, or events that could have led to such a release under slightly different conditions. The classification follows a tier system, where Tier 1 represents incidents with greater consequences, indicating the most significant lagging performance indicators, and Tier 2 reflects incidents of lesser consequence.

## Own workforce

OMV Petrom's sustainable business is built on trustworthy labor relations, on employees' knowledge engagement and commitment.

We aim for good employment conditions, fair and equal labor relations favorable to performance and healthy relations among employees and employer. We prioritize the health and safety of our employees and constantly take measures to prevent or mitigate adverse impacts from our operations.

We aim to respect and protect human rights in line with the Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR), UN Global Compact Principles, and International Labor Organization

(ILO) guidelines. We operate according to our fundamental responsibilities in upholding human rights and labor rights.

Our personnel policy centers on long-term employment, flexible working conditions, well-paid jobs and development opportunities, that raise engagement to the mutual benefit between staff and the organization. Recognizing the significance of job security for individuals, society, and the region, we fulfill our responsibilities through contingency planning. The projects and programs sustaining the company's developments are conceived and executed based on participative management and in constant alignment with employees' representatives on aspects that may affect employee's interests.
